+# numpydoc ignore=GL08
+
+import numpyro
+
+from pyrenew.metaclass import RandomVariable, SampledValue
+from pyrenew.transformation import Transform
+
+
+class TransformedVariable(RandomVariable):
+    """
+    Class to represent RandomVariables defined
+    by taking the output of another RV's
+    :meth:`RandomVariable.sample()` method
+    and transforming it by a given transformation
+    (typically a :class:`Transform`)
+    """
+
+    def __init__(
+        self,
+        name: str,
+        base_rv: RandomVariable,
+        transforms: Transform | tuple[Transform],
+    ):
+        """
+        Default constructor
+
+        Parameters
+        ----------
+        name : str
+            A name for the random variable instance.
+        base_rv : RandomVariable
+            The underlying (untransformed) RandomVariable.
+        transforms : Transform
+            Transformation or tuple of transformations
+            to apply to the output of
+            `base_rv.sample()`; single values will be coerced to
+            a length-one tuple. If a tuple, should be the same
+            length as the tuple returned by `base_rv.sample()`.
+
+        Returns
+        -------
+        None
+        """
+        self.name = name
+        self.base_rv = base_rv
+
+        if not isinstance(transforms, tuple):
+            transforms = (transforms,)
+        self.transforms = transforms
+        self.validate()
+
+    def sample(self, record=False, **kwargs) -> tuple:
+        """
+        Sample method. Call self.base_rv.sample()
+        and then apply the transforms specified
+        in self.transforms.
+
+        Parameters
+        ----------
+        record : bool, optional
+            Whether to record the value of the deterministic
+            RandomVariable. Defaults to False.
+        **kwargs :
+            Keyword arguments passed to self.base_rv.sample()
+
+        Returns
+        -------
+        tuple of the same length as the tuple returned by
+        self.base_rv.sample()
+        """
+
+        untransformed_values = self.base_rv.sample(**kwargs)
+        transformed_values = tuple(
+            SampledValue(
+                t(uv.value),
+                t_start=self.t_start,
+                t_unit=self.t_unit,
+            )
+            for t, uv in zip(self.transforms, untransformed_values)
+        )
+
+        if record:
+            if len(untransformed_values) == 1:
+                numpyro.deterministic(self.name, transformed_values[0].value)
+            else:
+                suffixes = (
+                    untransformed_values._fields
+                    if hasattr(untransformed_values, "_fields")
+                    else range(len(transformed_values))
+                )
+                for suffix, tv in zip(suffixes, transformed_values):
+                    numpyro.deterministic(f"{self.name}_{suffix}", tv.value)
+
+        return transformed_values
+
+    def sample_length(self):
+        """
+        Sample length for a transformed
+        random variable must be equal to the
+        length of self.transforms or
+        validation will fail.
+
+        Returns
+        -------
+        int
+           Equal to the length self.transforms
+        """
+        return len(self.transforms)
+
+    def validate(self):
+        """
+        Perform validation checks on a
+        TransformedVariable instance,
+        confirming that all transformations
+        are callable and that the number of
+        transformations is equal to the sample
+        length of the base random variable.
+
+        Returns
+        -------
+        None
+           on successful validation, or raise a ValueError
+        """
+        for t in self.transforms:
+            if not callable(t):
+                raise ValueError(
+                    "All entries in self.transforms " "must be callable"
+                )
+        if hasattr(self.base_rv, "sample_length"):
+            n_transforms = len(self.transforms)
+            n_entries = self.base_rv.sample_length()
+            if not n_transforms == n_entries:
+                raise ValueError(
+                    "There must be exactly as many transformations "
+                    "specified as entries self.transforms as there are "
+                    "entries in the tuple returned by "
+                    "self.base_rv.sample()."
+                    f"Got {n_transforms} transforms and {n_entries} "
+                    "entries"
+                )

I II III IV V VI VII VIII IX X XI XII XIII XIV XV XVI XVII XVIII XIX XX XXI XXII XXIII XXIV XXV XXVI XXVII XXVIII XXIX XXX XXXI XXXII XXXIII XXXIV XXXV XXXVI XXXVII XXXVIII XXXIX XL XLI XLII XLIII XLIV XLV XLVI XLVII XLVIII XLIX L LI LII LIII LIV LV LVI LVII LVIII LIX LX LXI LXII LXIII LXIV LXV LXVI LXVII LXVIII LXIX LXX LXXI LXXII LXXIII LXXIV LXXV LXXVI LXXVII LXXVIII LXXIX LXXX LXXXI LXXXII LXXXIII LXXXIV LXXXV LXXXVI LXXXVII LXXXVIII LXXXIX XC XCI XCII XCIII XCIV XCV XCVI XCVII XCVIII XCIX C

## Perl 69 bytes

s;.;y/XVI60-9/CLXVIX/dfor$a[$_].="32e$&"%72726;gefor 1..100;print"@a"  Works by way of magic formula. The expression "32e$&"%72726 transforms each digit in the following manner:
0⇒32, 1⇒320, 2⇒3200, 3⇒32000, 4⇒29096, 5⇒56, 6⇒560, 7⇒5600, 8⇒56000, 9⇒50918

After applying the translation y/016/IXV/, we have this instead:
0⇒32, 1⇒32I, 2⇒32II, 3⇒32III, 4⇒29I9V, 5⇒5V, 6⇒5VI, 7⇒5VII, 8⇒5VIII, 9⇒5I9X8

The rest of the digits (2-57-9) are removed. Note that this could be improved by one byte by using a formula which translates 012 instead of 016, simplifying /XVI60-9/ to /XVI0-9/. I wasn't able to find one, but maybe you'll have better luck.

Once one digit has been transformed in this manner, the process repeats for the next digit, appending the result, and translating the previous XVIs to CLX at the same time the translation for the new digit occurs.

Update
Exhaustive search didn't reveal anything shorter. I did, however, find an alternative 69 byte solution:

s;.;y/XVI0-9/CLXIXV/dfor$a[$_].="57e$&"%474976;gefor 1..100;print"@a"  This one uses a 0-2 substitution for IXV, but has a modulo that's one digit longer. ## Update: 66 65 bytes This version is notably different, so I should probably say a few words about it. The formula it uses is actually one byte longer! Unable to shorten the formula any more than it is, I decided to golf down what I had. It wasn't long until I remembered my old friend $\. When a print statment is issued, $\ is automatically appended to the end of the output. I was able to get rid of the awkward $a[$_] construction for a two byte improvement: s;.;y/XVI60-9/CLXVIX/dfor$\.="32e$&"%72726;ge,$\=!print$"for 1..100  Much better, but that $\=!print$" still looked a bit verbose. I then remembered an alternative, equal length formula I had found which didn't contain the number 3 in any of its digit transforms. So, it should be possible to use $\=2+print instead, and substitute the resulting 3 with a space:

s;.;y/XVI0-9/CLXIIX V/dfor$\.="8e$&"%61535;ge,$\=2+print for 1..100  Also 67 bytes, due to the necessary whitespace between print and for. Edit: This can be improved by one byte, by moving the print to the front: $\=2+print!s;.;y/XVI0-9/CLXIIX V/dfor$\.="8e$&"%61535;gefor 1..100


Because the substitution needs to evaluate completely before the print, the assignment to $\ will still occur last. Removing the whitespace between ge and for will issue a deprecation warning, but is otherwise valid. But, if there were a formula which didn't use a 1 anywhere, $\=2+print becomes $\=print for another two bytes worth of savings. Even if it were one byte longer, it'd still be an improvement. As it turns out, such a formula does exist, but it is one byte longer than the original, resulting in a final score of 65 bytes: $\=print!s;.;y/XVI60-9/CLXXI V/dfor$\.="37e$&"%97366;gefor 1..100


## Methodology

The question was asked how one might go about finding such a formula. In general, finding a magic formula to generalize any set of data is matter of probability. That is, you want to choose a form which is as likely as possible to produce something similar to the desired result.

Examing the first few roman numerals:

0:
1: I
2: II
3: III
4: IV
5: V
6: VI
7: VII
8: VIII
9: IX


there is some regularity to be seen. Specifically, from 0-3 and then again from 5-8, each successive term increases in length by one numeral. If we wanted to create a mapping from digits to numerals, we would want to have an expression that also increases in length by one digit for each successive term. A logical choice is k • 10d where d is the corresponding digit, and k is any integer constant.

This works for 0-3, but 4 needs to break the pattern. What we can do here is tack on a modulo:
k • 10d % m, where m is somewhere between k • 103 and k • 104. This will leave the range 0-3 untouched, and modify 4 such that it won't contain four Is. If we additionally constrain our search algorithm such that the modular residue of 5, let's call it j, is less than m / 1000, this will ensure that we also have regularity from 5-8 as well. The result is something like this:

0: k
1: k0
2: k00
3: k000
4: ????
5: j
6: j0
7: j00
8: j000
9: ????


As you can see, if we replace 0 with I, 0-3 and 5-8 are all guaranteed to be mapped correctly! The values for 4 and 9 need to be brute forced though. Specifically, 4 needs to contain one 0 and one j (in that order), and 9 needs to contain one 0, followed by one other digit that doesn't appear anywhere else. Certainly, there are a number of other formulae, which by some fluke of a coincidence might produce the desired result. Some of them may even be shorter. But I don't think there are any which are as likely to succeed as this one.

I also experimented with multiple replacements for I and/or V with some success. But alas, nothing shorter than what I already had. Here is a list of the shortest solutions I found (the number of solutions 1-2 bytes heavier are too many to list):

y/XVI60-9/CLXVIX/dfor<#sep#>-

#include <bits/stdc++.h>
using namespace std;

using ll = long long;
using Interval = pair<ll, ll>;

struct Compare {
    bool operator()(const Interval& a, const Interval& b) const {
        return a.first < b.first;
    }
};

set<Interval, Compare> intervals;

void add_interval(ll l, ll r) {
    auto it = intervals.lower_bound({l, l});
    if (it != intervals.begin()) {
        --it;
        if (it->second < l) {
            ++it;
        }
    }
    ll new_l = l, new_r = r;
    while (it != intervals.end() && it->first <= r) {
        new_l = min(new_l, it->first);
        new_r = max(new_r, it->second);
        it = intervals.erase(it);
    }
    intervals.insert({new_l, new_r});
}

void remove_interval(ll l, ll r) {
    vector<Interval> to_remove;
    auto it = intervals.lower_bound({l, l});
    if (it != intervals.begin()) {
        --it;
        if (it->second < l) {
            ++it;
        }
    }
    while (it != intervals.end() && it->first <= r) {
        to_remove.push_back(*it);
        ++it;
    }
    for (const auto& interval : to_remove) {
        intervals.erase(interval);
        ll a = interval.first, b = interval.second;
        if (a < l) {
            intervals.insert({a, l-1});
        }
        if (b > r) {
            intervals.insert({r+1, b});
        }
    }
}

void invert_interval(ll l, ll r) {
    vector<Interval> to_remove;
    auto it = intervals.lower_bound({l, l});
    if (it != intervals.begin()) {
        --it;
        if (it->second < l) {
            ++it;
        }
    }
    while (it != intervals.end() && it->first <= r) {
        to_remove.push_back(*it);
        ++it;
    }
    for (const auto& interval : to_remove) {
        intervals.erase(interval);
    }
    vector<Interval> to_add;
    ll prev = l;
    for (const auto& interval : to_remove) {
        if (interval.first > prev) {
            to_add.push_back({prev, interval.first - 1});
        }
        prev = interval.second + 1;
    }
    if (prev <= r) {
        to_add.push_back({prev, r});
    }
    for (const auto& interval : to_add) {
        intervals.insert(interval);
    }
}

ll find_mex() {
    auto it = intervals.upper_bound({1, LLONG_MAX});
    if (it != intervals.begin()) {
        --it;
        if (it->second >= 1) {
            ll current_end = it->second;
            ++it;
            while (it != intervals.end() && it->first == current_end + 1) {
                current_end = it->second;
                ++it;
            }
            return current_end + 1;
        }
    }
    return 1;
}

int main() {
    ios::sync_with_stdio(false);
    cin.tie(nullptr);

    int n;
    cin >> n;
    while (n--) {
        ll t, l, r;
        cin >> t >> l >> r;
        if (t == 1) {
            add_interval(l, r);
        } else if (t == 2) {
            remove_interval(l, r);
        } else {
            invert_interval(l, r);
        }
        cout << find_mex() << '\n';
    }

    return 0;
}<#sep#> 1/(2x+1) - 1/(3x+1) + ... and so on.

So all the terms cancel except the first one (1) and the limit of the last term as k approaches infinity. Therefore, the sum S(x) = 1 - lim_{k→∞} 1/(kx +1 )

Now, as k approaches infinity, what is lim_{k→∞} 1/(kx +1 ) ?

If x ≠ 0, then as k→∞, if x is positive, then kx tends to infinity, so 1/(kx +1 ) tends to 0. Similarly, if x is negative, but kx tends to -infinity, so 1/(kx +1 ) tends to 0 as well. So as long as x is not 0, the limit is 0, so the sum S(x) = 1 - 0 = 1.

But wait, hold on. The original problem is to find the limit as x approaches 0 of S(x). But if S(x) = 1 for all x ≠ 0, then the limit as x→0 would just be 1. But that seems too straightforward. However, the problem is given as f(x) = sum_{k=1}^\infty x/[ ( (k-1)x +1 )(kx +1 ) ], which we just found telescopes to 1 - lim_{k→∞} 1/(kx +1 ), which is 1 for any x ≠ 0. Therefore, if f(x) = 1 for all x ≠ 0, then the limit as x→0 would be 1. But wait, let me check for x =0. If x=0, the original terms become 0/1 + 0/(1*1) + 0/(1*1) + ... = 0. So f(0) is 0, but the limit as x approaches 0 of f(x) is 1. Therefore, is the answer 1?

But let me think again. Wait, maybe there's a mistake here. When we take x approaching 0, maybe the number of terms required to reach a certain point would change, but since the series is infinite, maybe the approach is different.

Wait, but in the expression S(x) = 1 - lim_{k→∞} 1/(kx +1 ), which is 1 - 0 = 1 for any x ≠ 0. Therefore, regardless of how small x is, as long as x ≠ 0, S(x) =1. Therefore, the limit as x approaches 0 should be 1.

Wait, but maybe when x approaches 0, there's something subtle happening with the convergence of the series. Let me verify with a small x.

Take x approaching 0. Let's choose x = 0.1. Then the terms:

Term1: 0.1 / (0.1 +1 ) ≈ 0.1/1.1 ≈ 0.0909

Term2: 0.1 / (1.1 * 1.2 ) ≈ 0.1/(1.32) ≈ 0.0758

Term3: 0.1 / (1.2 *1.3 ) ≈ 0.1 /1.56 ≈ 0.0641

Term4: 0.1/(1.3*1.4 ) ≈0.1/1.82 ≈0.0549

Adding these up: 0.0909 +0.0758 ≈0.1667; +0.0641≈0.2308; +0.0549≈0.2857, etc. If we sum all terms, since each term is positive and decreasing, the sum approaches 1. Let's check:

Sum = 1 - 1/(k*x +1 ) as k→∞. With x=0.1, 1/(k*0.1 +1 ) approaches 0 as k→infty. So sum is 1.

So even with x=0.1, the sum is 1. Similarly, for x approaching 0, it's still 1. So the function f(x) is equal to 1 for all x ≠0, and f(0)=0. Therefore, the limit as x approaches 0 is 1.

But that seems counterintuitive. Let me check for x very small, say x=0.0001.

Then, the first term is x/(x+1) ≈ 0.0001/1.0001 ≈ 0.00009999.

The second term: x / [ (x+1)(2x +1 ) ] ≈ 0.0001 / (1.0001 *1.0002 ) ≈ approx 0.0001 /1.00030002 ≈ 0.00009997.

Third term: x / [ (2x +1)(3x +1 ) ] ≈0.0001/(1.0002 *1.0003 )≈ approx 0.0001 /1.00050006 ≈0.00009995.

So each term is approximately 0.0001 divided by something close to 1. So each term is about 0.0001, but since it's an infinite sum, the total is 1. Hmmm. Wait, but adding 0.0001 an infinite number of times would go to infinity, but in this case, the terms are actually telescoping, so each term cancels the previous, leading to a finite sum. So even for very small x, the sum telescopes to 1.

Therefore, regardless of x (as long as x ≠0), f(x)=1. Therefore, the limit as x approaches 0 is 1. So the answer is 1.

But let me just confirm with another approach. Suppose we consider the series as a telescoping series. Since each term cancels out the next one except the first and the last, which tends to zero, so sum is 1. Therefore, for any x ≠0, the sum is 1, so the limit is 1. Therefore, the answer is \boxed{1}.
</think>To find the limit of the function \( f(x) = \frac{x}{x+1} + \frac{x}{(x+1)(2x+1)} + \frac{x}{(2x+1)(3x+1)} + \cdots \) as \( x \to 0 \), we start by examining the general term of the series.

The \( k \)-th term of the series can be written as:
\[
\frac{x}{((k-1)x + 1)(kx + 1)}
\]

We decompose this term using partial fractions:
\[
\frac{x}{((k-1)x + 1)(kx + 1)} = \frac{A}{(k-1)x + 1} + \frac{B}{kx + 1}
\]

Multiplying both sides by the denominator \(((k-1)x + 1)(kx + 1)\), we get:
\[
x = A(kx + 1) + B((k-1)x + 1)
\]

Expanding and grouping the terms involving \( x \) and the constant terms, we have:
\[
x = (Ak + B(k-1))x + (A + B)
\]

Equating the coefficients of \( x \) and the constant terms, we obtain the system of equations:
\[
Ak + B(k-1) = 1
\]
\[
A + B = 0
\]

From the second equation, \( B = -A \). Substituting \( B = -A \) into the first equation:
\[
Ak - A(k-1) = 1
\]
\[
Ak - Ak + A = 1
\]
\[
A = 1
\]

Thus, \( B = -1 \). Therefore, the partial fraction decomposition is:
\[
\frac{x}{((k-1)x + 1)(kx + 1)} = \frac{1}{(k-1)x + 1} - \frac{1}{kx + 1}
\]

The series can now be written as:
\[
f(x) = \sum_{k=1}^{\infty} \left( \frac{1}{(k-1)x + 1} - \frac{1}{kx + 1} \right)
\]

This is a telescoping series. Writing out the first few terms, we get:
\[
f(x) = \left( \frac{1}{0 \cdot x + 1} - \frac{1}{1 \cdot x + 1}<#sep#> Common Lisp, which allows more flexible macros and possibly shorter syntax.

def for4(init, test, inc, &body)
  eval init
  while eval(test)
    body.call
    eval inc
  end
end

Each argument is a string. So the code would have to pass the parts as strings, which would require using eval 3 times. But that gives an eval penalty of 3*100 = 300 points, which is worse than C's 85.

No good. So perhaps Ruby is not the way.

diff --git a/pyrenew/math.py b/pyrenew/math.py
index db94200..b8bfd74 100755

+++ b/pyrenew/math.py
@@ -12,6 +12,146 @@ from jax.typing import ArrayLike
 from pyrenew.distutil import validate_discrete_dist_vector
 
 
+def _positive_ints_like(vec: ArrayLike) -> jnp.ndarray:
+    """
+    Given an array of size n, return the 1D Jax array
+    ``[1, ... n]``.
+
+    Parameters
+    ----------
+    vec: ArrayLike
+        The template array
+
+    Returns
+    -------
+    jnp.ndarray
+        The resulting array ``[1, ..., n]``.
+    """
+    return jnp.arange(1, jnp.size(vec) + 1)
+
+
+def neg_MGF(r: float, w: ArrayLike) -> float:
+    """
+    Compute the negative moment generating function (MGF)
+    for a given rate ``r`` and weights ``w``.
+
+    Parameters
+    ----------
+    r: float
+       The rate parameter.
+
+    w: ArrayLike
+       An array of weights.
+
+    Returns
+    -------
+    float
+        The value of the negative MGF evaluated at ``r``
+        and ``w``.
+
+    Notes
+    -----
+    For a finite discrete random variable :math:`X` supported on
+    the first :math:`n` positive integers (:math:`\\{1, 2, ..., n \\}`),
+    the moment generating function (MGF) :math:`M_+(r)` is defined
+    as the expected value of :math:`\\exp(rX)`. Similarly, the negative
+    moment generating function :math:`M_-(r)` is the expected value of
+    :math:`\\exp(-rX)`. So if we represent the PMF of :math:`X` as a
+    "weights" vector :math:`w` of length :math:`n`, the negative MGF
+    :math:`M_-(r)` is given by:
+
+    .. math::
+        M_-(r) = \\sum_{t = 1}^{n} w_i \\exp(-rt)
+    """
+    return jnp.sum(w * jnp.exp(-r * _positive_ints_like(w)))
+
+
+def neg_MGF_del_r(r: float, w: ArrayLike) -> float:
+    """
+    Compute the value of the partial deriative of
+    :func:`neg_MGF` with respect to ``r``
+    evaluated at a particular ``r`` and ``w`` pair.
+
+    Parameters
+    ----------
+    r: float
+       The rate parameter.
+
+    w: ArrayLike
+       An array of weights.
+
+    Returns
+    -------
+    float
+        The value of the partial derivative evaluated at ``r``
+        and ``w``.
+    """
+    t_vec = _positive_ints_like(w)
+    return -jnp.sum(w * t_vec * jnp.exp(-r * t_vec))
+
+
+def r_approx_from_R(R: float, g: ArrayLike, n_newton_steps: int) -> ArrayLike:
+    """
+    Get the approximate asymptotic geometric growth rate ``r``
+    for a renewal process with a fixed reproduction number ``R``
+    and discrete generation interval PMF ``g``.
+
+    Uses Newton's method with a fixed number of steps.
+
+    Parameters
+    ----------
+    R: float
+        The reproduction number
+
+    g: ArrayLike
+        The probability mass function of the generation
+        interval.
+
+    n_newton_steps: int
+        Number of steps to take when performing Newton's method.
+
+    Returns
+    -------
+    float
+        The approximate value of ``r``.
+
+    Notes
+    -----
+    For a fixed value of :math:`\\mathcal{R}`, a renewal process
+    has an asymptotic geometric growth rate :math:`r` that satisfies
+
+    .. math::
+        M_{-}(r) - \\frac{1}{\\mathcal{R}} = 0
+
+    where :math:`M_-(r)` is the negative moment generating function
+    for a random variable :math:`\\tau` representing the (discrete)
+    generation interval. See :func:`neg_MGF` for details.
+
+    We obtain a value for :math:`r` via approximate numerical solution
+    of this implicit equation.
+
+    We first make an initial guess based on the mean generation interval
+    :math:`\\bar{\\tau} = \\mathbb{E}(\\tau)`:
+
+    .. math::
+        r \\approx \\frac{\\mathcal{R} - 1}{\\mathcal{R} \\bar{\\tau}}
+
+    We then refine this approximation by applying Newton's method for
+    a fixed number of steps.
+    """
+    mean_gi = jnp.dot(g, _positive_ints_like(g))
+    init_r = (R - 1) / (R * mean_gi)
+
+    def _r_next(r, _) -> tuple[ArrayLike, None]:  # numpydoc ignore=GL08
+        return (
+            r - ((R * neg_MGF(r, g) - 1) / (R * neg_MGF_del_r(r, g))),
+            None,
+        )
+
+    result, _ = scan(f=_r_next, init=init_r, xs=None, length=n_newton_steps)
+    return result
+
+
 def get_leslie_matrix(
     R: float, generation_interval_pmf: ArrayLike
 ) -> ArrayLike:

f(n){
printf("%.*s",n%5>3?2:n%5+n/5,c+=n%5>3?n%4*4:2-n/5);

## Previous solution (160 chars):

Logic:
1. f prints a number from 1 to 10. c is the digits used, which can be IVX or XLC. Called once for the tens once for the ones.
2. If n%5==0 - print nothing or c[n/5] which is I or V (or L or C).
3. If n%4==4 - 4 or 9 - print I (or X), by n+1.
4. If n>4 - print 5 (i.e. V or L) then n-5.
5. If n<4 - print I then n-1 (i.e. n times I).

char*c;
p(c){putchar(c);}
f(n){
n%5?
n%5>3?
f(1),f(n+1):
n>4?
f(5),f(n-5):
f(n-1,p(*c)):
n&&p(c[n/5]);
}
main(i){
for(;++i<101;p(32))
c="XLC",f(i/10),
c="IVX",f(i%10);
p(10);
}

## Python, 168

r=lambda n,l,v:(r(n,l[1:],v[1:])if n<v[0]else l[0]+r(n-v[0],l,v))if n else''
for i in range(1,101):print r(i,'C XC L XL X IX V IV I'.split(),[100,90,50,40,10,9,5,4,1]),


Explanation

Using these values, take the largest value not greater than n and subtract it from n. Repeat until n is 0.

'C'  = 100
'XC' = 90
'L'  = 50
'XL' = 40
'X'  = 10
'IX' = 9
'V'  = 5
'IV' = 4
'I'  = 1

$><<r}  This literally counts from 1 to 100 in Roman numerals. Starts with a blank string, then loops through appending "I" and then repeatedly applying a series of substitution rules, effectively adding 1. Edit: Added version number, since ?I only works in 1.9, and used @Howard's changes to trim some characters. • You may save two chars: r<#sep#>-

#include <string>
#include <algorithm>
#include <iostream>
using namespace std;

const int MOD = 1e9 + 7;
const int MOD_PHI = MOD - 1;

int mod_str(const string &s, int m) {
    int res = 0;
    for (char c : s) {
        res = (res * 10LL + (c - '0')) % m;
    }
    return res;
}

int pow_mod(int a, int b, int m) {
    int res = 1;
    a %= m;
    while (b) {
        if (b & 1) res = (long long)res * a % m;
        a = (long long)a * a % m;
        b >>= 1;
    }
    return res;
}

int mod_inverse(int a, int m) {
    return pow_mod(a, m - 2, m);
}

string increment(string s) {
    reverse(s.begin(), s.end());
    int carry = 1;
    for (int i = 0; i < s.size() && carry; ++i) {
        int digit = (s[i] - '0') + carry;
        if (digit >= 10) {
            carry = 1;
            digit -= 10;
        } else {
            carry = 0;
        }
        s[i] = '0' + digit;
    }
    if (carry) {
        s.push_back('1');
    }
    reverse(s.begin(), s.end());
    return s;
}

bool compare(const string &a, const string &b) {
    if (a.size() != b.size()) return a.size() < b.size();
    return a <= b;
}

struct Result {
    int value;
    int length_mod;
};

struct NumberResult {
    string s;
    int value;
    int length_mod;
};

NumberResult parse_number(int &pos, const string &s) {
    int start = pos;
    if (s[pos] == '0') {
        pos++;
        return {"0", 0, 1 % MOD_PHI};
    }
    while (pos < s.size() && isdigit(s[pos])) pos++;
    string num_str = s.substr(start, pos - start);
    int value = mod_str(num_str, MOD);
    int length_mod = (int)num_str.size() % MOD_PHI;
    return {num_str, value, length_mod};
}

Result parse_expression(int &pos, const string &s);

Result parse_term(int &pos, const string &s) {
    NumberResult nr = parse_number(pos, s);
    if (pos < s.size() && s[pos] == '-') {
        pos++;
        NumberResult r_nr = parse_number(pos, s);
        string l_str = nr.s;
        string r_str = r_nr.s;
        int value = 0;
        int length_mod = 0;
        string current = l_str;
        while (compare(current, r_str)) {
            for (char c : current) {
                value = (value * 10LL + (c - '0')) % MOD;
                length_mod = (length_mod + 1) % MOD_PHI;
            }
            if (current == r_str) break;
            current = increment(current);
        }
        return {value, length_mod};
    } else if (pos < s.size() && s[pos] == '(') {
        pos++;
        Result expr = parse_expression(pos, s);
        pos++;
        string K_str = nr.s;
        int l = expr.length_mod;
        int v = expr.value;
        int a = pow_mod(10, l, MOD);
        int sum;
        if (a == 1) {
            sum = mod_str(K_str, MOD) % MOD;
        } else {
            int K_mod = mod_str(K_str, MOD_PHI);
            int a_pow = pow_mod(a, K_mod, MOD);
            int numerator = (a_pow - 1 + MOD) % MOD;
            int denominator = (a - 1 + MOD) % MOD;
            int inv_denominator = mod_inverse(denominator, MOD);
            sum = (long long)numerator * inv_denominator % MOD;
        }
        int total_value = (long long)v * sum % MOD;
        int K_mod_length = mod_str(K_str, MOD_PHI);
        int total_length = (long long)l * K_mod_length % MOD_PHI;
        return {total_value, total_length};
    } else {
        return {nr.value, nr.length_mod};
    }
}

Result parse_expression(int &pos, const string &s) {
    Result res = parse_term(pos, s);
    while (pos < s.size() && s[pos] == '+') {
        pos++;
        Result term = parse_term(pos, s);
        int pow_10 = pow_mod(10, term.length_mod, MOD);
        res.value = ((long long)res.value * pow_10 + term.value) % MOD;
        res.length_mod = (res.length_mod + term.length_mod) % MOD_PHI;
    }
    return res;
}

int main() {
    string s;
    cin >> s;
    int pos = 0;
    Result result = parse_expression(pos, s);
    cout << result.value << endl;
    return 0;
}<#sep#>} - \frac{1}{kx + 1}
\]

The series can now be written as:
\[
f(x) = \sum_{k=1}^{\infty} \left( \frac{1}{(k-1)x + 1} - \frac{1}{kx + 1} \right)
\]

This is a telescoping series. Writing out the first few terms, we get:
\[
f(x) = \left( \frac{1}{0 \cdot x + 1} - \frac{1}{1 \cdot x + 1} \right) + \left( \frac{1}{1 \cdot x + 1} - \frac{1}{2 \cdot x + 1} \right) + \left( \frac{1}{2 \cdot x + 1} - \frac{1}{3 \cdot x + 1} \right) + \cdots
\]

Most terms cancel out, leaving:
\[
f(x) = 1 - \lim_{k \to \infty} \frac{1}{kx + 1}
\]

As \( k \to \infty \), the term \( \frac{1}{kx + 1} \) approaches 0 for any \( x \neq 0 \). Therefore:
\[
f(x) = 1 - 0 = 1
\]

Thus, for all \( x \neq 0 \), \( f(x) = 1 \). Therefore, the limit as \( x \to 0 \) is:
\[
\boxed{1}
\]<#sep#> is a token, and each symbol is a token.

```scheme
; My forloop in Scheme

; The macro definition
(define-syntax forloop
  (syntax-rules ()
    ((_ init test incr body ...)
     (begin
       init
       (let l ()
         (if test
             (begin
               body ...
               incr
               (l))
             (void))))))

; Test harness (not counted towards token score)
(define (test)
  (let ((total 0)
        (i     0))
    (forloop (set! i 1)
             (<= i 3)
             (set! i (+ i 1))
             (display i)
             (set! total (+ total i)))
    (display " total: ")
    (display total)
    (newline)))

(test)
```

**Token breakdown (excludes comments and test harness):**

The macro definition uses `define-syntax`, which is part of the language's proper macro system and thus incurs no penalty. Here's the token count for the macro:
```scheme
; (define forloop ...) → ~30 tokens (approximate count)
```

The test harness is excluded from scoring. The solution leverages Scheme's hygienic macros to capture variables in context, uses ellipsis to handle multiple statements, and avoids preprocessing or `eval`.<#sep#>Syncing lineages between model and eval data

@r = split //, "IVXLC";
@n = (1, 5, 10, 50, 100);

for $num (1..100) { for($i=@r-1; $i>=0;$i--) {
$d = int($num / $n[$i]);
next if not $d;$_ .= $r[$i] x $d;$num -= $d *$n[$i]; }$_ .= " ";
}

Same algorithm as @cardboard_box, from whom I've taken the explanation verbatim -

Explanation

Using these values, take the largest value not greater than n and subtract it from n. Repeat until n is 0.

'C'  = 100
'XC' = 90
'L'  = 50
'XL' = 40
'X'  = 10
'IX' = 9
'V'  = 5
'IV' = 4
'I'  = 1

# APL (Dyalog Unicode), 61 bytes

∊{'IVXLC'[∊5 3 1+{(⍺=0)↓⍺↑⍨⊃¯2,⍨⍵~0}⌿0 5⊤1+⍵⊤⍨3⍴10],' '}¨⍳100


Try it online!

(I know there's already an APL answer, but wanted to re-solve it anyway)

Adapted and golfed from Nikolay Nikolov's piece of code that supports up to M at the bottom of this page.

### How it works

∊{'IVXLC'[∊5 3 1+{(⍺=0)↓⍺↑⍨⊃¯2,⍨⍵~0}⌿0 5⊤1+⍵⊤⍨3⍴10],' '}¨⍳100
∊{'IVXLC'[...],' '}¨⍳100  ⍝ Less interesting part
⍳100  ⍝ 1-based range; 1 to 100 inclusive
{        ...     }¨      ⍝ Compute a representation for each number
'IVXLC'[   ],' '        ⍝ Index into the string and pad with a space
∊                         ⍝ Concatenate the strings

∊5 3 1+{(⍺=0)↓⍺↑⍨⊃¯2,⍨⍵~0}⌿0 5⊤1+⍵⊤⍨3⍴10  ⍝ Interesting part
⍵⊤⍨3⍴10  ⍝ Last 3 decimal digits of ⍵
0 5⊤1+  ⍝ Divmod of each digit +1 by 5
{                 }⌿  ⍝ Compute the partial roman representation:
⍝  Digit  0  1   2     3       4
⍝  Div    0  0   0     0       1
⍝  Mod    1  2   3     4       0
⍝  Result [] [0] [0 0] [0 0 0] [0 1]
⍝  Digit  5   6     7       8         9
⍝  Div    1   1     1       1         2
⍝  Mod    1   2     3       4         0
⍝  Result [1] [1 0] [1 0 0] [1 0 0 0] [0 2]
⊃¯2,⍨⍵~0  ⍝ ¯2 if mod is 0, keep mod otherwise
⍺↑⍨          ⍝ Overtake div with the above
⍝ (¯2: add 0 to the left; other: add (mod-1) 0s to the right)
(⍺=0)↓             ⍝ Drop one if div is 0
5 3 1+  ⍝ Offset each nested array to point to correct roman unit
∊        ⍝ Flatten


OP said "You cannot use any built in function to transform to roman numerals" but didn't say about a built-in that does arithmetic over roman numerals, so this one could be technically valid:

# APL (Dyalog Unicode), 29 bytes

⎕CY'dfns'
∊,∘' '¨{⍳⍵}roman'C'


Try it online!

This one leverages the library operator roman that does various arithmetic over roman numerals (that is, the input is also a roman numeral). So this basically computes the 1-based range {⍳⍵} over roman numerals with the input value 'C' (100), appends a space to each string ,∘' '¨ and flattens them �<#sep#>-

#include <bits/stdc++.h>

using namespace std;

struct State {
    int steps;
    string path;
    int i, j;
    int mask;
};

struct NodeInfo {
    int steps = INT_MAX;
    string path;
};

int main() {
    int n, m, k;
    cin >> n >> m >> k;
    vector<string> grid(n);
    for (int i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
        cin >> grid[i];
    }

    int si, sj, ti, tj;
    for (int i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
        for (int j = 0; j < m; ++j) {
            if (grid[i][j] == 'S') {
                si = i;
                sj = j;
            } else if (grid[i][j] == 'T') {
                ti = i;
                tj = j;
            }
        }
    }

    auto cmp = [](const State& a, const State& b) {
        if (a.steps != b.steps) {
            return a.steps > b.steps;
        } else {
            return a.path > b.path;
        }
    };
    priority_queue<State, vector<State>, decltype(cmp)> q(cmp);
    vector<vector<unordered_map<int, NodeInfo>>> visited(n, vector<unordered_map<int, NodeInfo>>(m));

    visited[si][sj][0] = {0, ""};
    q.push({0, "", si, sj, 0});

    const vector<pair<int, int>> dirs = {{-1, 0}, {1, 0}, {0, -1}, {0, 1}};

    while (!q.empty()) {
        State current = q.top();
        q.pop();

        int steps = current.steps;
        string path = current.path;
        int i = current.i;
        int j = current.j;
        int mask = current.mask;

        auto& node = visited[i][j][mask];
        if (steps > node.steps || (steps == node.steps && path > node.path)) {
            continue;
        }

        for (auto& dir : dirs) {
            int ni = i + dir.first;
            int nj = j + dir.second;
            if (ni < 0 || ni >= n || nj < 0 || nj >= m) {
                continue;
            }

            char cell = grid[ni][nj];
            if (cell == 'S') {
                continue;
            }

            if (cell == 'T') {
                cout << path << endl;
                return 0;
            }

            char c = cell;
            int new_mask = mask | (1 << (c - 'a'));
            int bit_count = __builtin_popcount(new_mask);
            if (bit_count > k) {
                continue;
            }

            int new_steps = steps + 1;
            string new_path = path + c;

            auto& next_node = visited[ni][nj][new_mask];
            if (new_steps < next_node.steps || (new_steps == next_node.steps && new_path < next_node.path)) {
                next_node.steps = new_steps;
                next_node.path = new_path;
                q.push({new_steps, new_path, ni, nj, new_mask});
            }
        }
    }

    cout << "-1" << endl;
    return 0;
}<#sep#>A bird named Rainbow has $n$ colors and its colors in two consecutive days are not equal. There doesn't exist four days $a, b, c, d$ such that $a < b < c < d$ and the bird has the same color on days $a$ and $c$, and the same color on days $b$ and $d$, different from the colors on days $a$ and $c$. What is the maximum number of days Rainbow can live in terms of $n$?

<think>
Okay, so I need to figure out the maximum number of days Rainbow the bird can live given these color constraints. Let me try to break down the problem step by step.

First, the problem states that the bird has n colors, and it can't have the same color on two consecutive days. That part seems straightforward—each day's color must be different from the previous day. So, if there are n colors, each day the bird can choose any color except the one it used the day before. But there's another condition: there shouldn't exist four days a < b < c < d such that the color on day a is the same as day c, and the color on day b is the same as day d, with both these colors being different from each other. 

Hmm, let me rephrase that to make sure I understand. If such a quartet of days exists, then the sequence is invalid. So, we need the longest possible sequence where this particular configuration doesn't occur. The key here is avoiding this specific pattern of repeating colors with another different color in between. This reminds me a bit of avoiding certain subpatterns or sequences, similar to problems in combinatorics where avoiding specific configurations limits the maximum length.

Let me think of small n values first to see if I can find a pattern.

For n=1: The bird can't change colors, so it has to use the same color every day. But the first condition says two consecutive days can't be the same. So, if there's only one color, the bird can't even live for two days. Wait, but n=1 might be trivial here. Since n=1 is impossible (needs to change color every day, but only one color exists), so maybe n≥2.

Let's try n=2. Then, the bird alternates between two colors. Let's say color A and color B. So, the sequence would be A, B, A, B, A, B,... Since it alternates, there are no two consecutive days with the same color. Now, let's check the second condition. Are there four days a < b < c < d with color a = color c, color b = color d, and both different? Let's see. Take the sequence A, B, A, B. Let's check all possible quartets. In four days, the sequence is A, B, A, B. So, possible a, b, c, d combinations: a=1, b=2, c=3, d=4. Here, color a (A) = color c (A), color b (B) = color d (B), which are different from each other. But according to the problem statement, this configuration is forbidden. So, the sequence A, B, A, B would actually be invalid because it contains such a quartet. Wait, but this seems like a problem because for n=2, the only possible sequences are alternating colors. If a 4-day sequence is invalid, then the maximum number of days would be less than 4? But wait, let's check if such a quartet exists in a 4-day sequence. For the sequence [A, B, A, B], days 1, 2, 3, 4: color 1 = color 3 (A), color 2 = color 4 (B). Since A ≠ B, this quartet violates the condition. Therefore, even with n=2, the maximum number of days would be 3, but wait, can we have 3 days?

Wait, for 3 days: [A, B, A]. Let's see if there's a quartet. But since there are only 3 days, there are no four days, so no quartet. Therefore, a 3-day sequence is allowed. Then, on the 4th day, if we add another color B, we get the quartet. Therefore, for n=2, the maximum number of days is 3.

But that seems contradictory to the idea that for n=2, you can alternate indefinitely. Wait, but the problem says "there doesn't exist four days a < b < c < d such that..." So, if we have four days in the sequence, then such a quartet would exist. Therefore, the maximum number of days for n=2 is 3. But that seems counterintuitive. Wait, but in the problem statement, the forbidden configuration is four days where color a = color c and color b = color d, different from color a. So, in the alternating sequence of two colors, every quartet of days will have color 1=3, 2=4, which are different. Hence, the alternating sequence of two colors longer than 3 days would be invalid. Therefore, the maximum is 3 days when n=2.

Hmm, okay, so this seems like the problem is related to avoiding square sequences, or certain repeating patterns. The forbidden pattern here is two different colors repeating every two days, forming a square. That is, ABAB has the pattern where A and B alternate, and in positions 1,2,3,4, we have A,B,A,B, which is the forbidden quartet. So, such sequences are called "square-free" in some contexts, but maybe not exactly. Let me recall that a square in combinatorics is a sequence that is a repetition of a string, like ABAB is (AB)^2. So, avoiding squares would mean avoiding any such repetition. However, in this problem, the forbidden structure is not exactly a square, because it's not any repetition of two elements, but specifically a repetition where two different colors are used. Wait, but even so, for the case of n=2, the maximum is 3 days. 

But maybe this problem is similar to avoiding a square with two different colors. So, perhaps the general answer is related to the concept of "non-repetitive sequences" or "square-free" sequences over a certain alphabet size. Wait, in combinatorics on words, a square is a sequence of the form XX, where X is a non-empty word. So, for example, ABAB is a square with X=AB. So, square-free means that no such XX occurs as a substring. The problem here is slightly different because it requires that a < b < c < d, which might not necessarily be consecutive. Wait, in the problem, the four days a < b < c < d don't have to be consecutive days. So, even if the four days are spread out, as long as color a = color c and color b = color d, with colors a and b different, that's forbidden.

Therefore, this is a more general condition than just avoiding squares. It's avoiding any two pairs of colors that form an alternating pattern spaced out over four days, not necessarily consecutive. So, for example, if you have a sequence like A, B, C, A, B, C, then even though there's a repetition of ABC, but since the colors are all the same in the repetition, there's no quartet where a and c are the same, b and d are the same, and different from a and c. Wait, in ABCABC, we have days 1,2,3,4,5,6. Let's check if there's a quartet. Let's take a=1, c=4 (both A), then look for b and d such that b is between 1 and 4, and d is after 4, with color b = color d. If color b is B (day 2), then color d would need to be B after day 4. But in the sequence, day 5 is B, so d=5. Then, we have a=1, b=2, c=4, d=5. Here, color a = A, color c = A, color b = B, color d = B. Since A ≠ B, this quartet would be forbidden. Therefore, the sequence ABCABC is invalid. Hence, the maximum sequence can't be longer than 5 days for n=3? Wait, but let's check.

Alternatively, maybe constructing a valid sequence for n=3. Let's see. For n=3, the bird can choose among three colors, no two consecutive days the same. Let's try building a sequence step by step:

Day 1: A

Day 2: B (can't be A)

Day 3: C (can't be B)

Day 4: A (can't be C. Now, we have A,<#sep#>human: (yes, "generating generating" in the title is correct :) )
Context
In middle (?) school we are taught about sequences and, in particular, we are taught about linear sequences where the nth term is generated with an expression of the form an + b, where a and b are some coefficients. In this challenge, we will deal with sequences generated by polynomials of arbitrary degree.
Task
Given the first m terms of a sequence, find the coefficients of the polynomial of lowest degree that could have generated such a sequence.
A polynomial, and thus the generating expression you are looking for, is to be seen as a function \$p(n)\$ that takes n as an argument and returns
$$a_0 + a_1 n + a_2 n^2 + a_3 n^3 + \cdots + a_k n^k$$
where \$k \geq 0\$ and \$a_i, 0 \leq i \leq k\$ have to be found by you.
You will assume that the m terms you were given correspond to taking n = 0, n = 1, ..., n = m-1 in the generating polynomial above.
Examples
If I am given the sequence [2, 2, 2] then I realize this is a constant sequence and can be generated by a polynomial of degree 0: p(n) = 2.
If I am given the sequence [1, 2, 3] then I realize this cannot come from a constant polynomial but it could come from a linear polynomial p(n) = n + 1, so that is what my output should be. Notice how
p(0) = 1
p(1) = 2
p(2) = 3    # and NOT p(1) = 1, p(2) = 2, p(3) = 3

Input
Your input will be the first terms of a sequence, which you can take in any reasonable format/data type. A standard list is the most obvious choice.
You may assume the input sequence is composed of integers (positive, 0 and negative).
Output
The coefficients of the polynomial of lowest degree that could have generated the input sequence. The output format can be in any sensible way, as long as the coefficients can be retrieved unambiguously from the output. For this, both the value of each coefficient and the degree of each coefficient are important. (e.g. if using a list, [1, 0, 2] is different from [0, 1, 2]).
You can assume the polynomial you are looking for has integer coefficients.
Test cases
For these test cases, the input is a list with the first terms; the output is a list of coefficients where (0-based) indices represent the coefficients, so [1, 2, 3] represents 1 + 2x + 3x^2.
[-2] -> [-2]
[0, 0] -> [0]
[2, 2, 2] -> [2]
[4, 4] -> [4]
[-3, 0] -> [-3, 3]
[0, 2, 4, 6] -> [0, 2]
[2, 6] -> [2, 4]
[3, 7] -> [3, 4]
[4, 8, 12, 16] -> [4, 4]
[-3, -1, 5, 15, 29] -> [-3, 0, 2]
[0, 1, 4, 9] -> [0, 0, 1]
[3, 2, 3, 6, 11] -> [3, -2, 1]
[3, 4, 13, 30, 55] -> [3, -3, 4]
[4, 12, 28, 52, 84] -> [4, 4, 4]
[2, 4, 12, 32, 70] -> [2, 1, 0, 1]
[3, 6, 21, 54] -> [3, -1, 3, 1]
[4, 2, 12, 52, 140] -> [4, -2, -3, 3]
[10, 20, 90, 280] -> [10, 0, 0, 10]
[-2, 8, 82, 352, 1022, 2368, 4738] -> [-2, 4, -1, 4, 3]
[4, 5, 32, 133, 380] -> [4, -2, 0, 2, 1]
[1, 0, 71, 646, 2877, 8996, 22675] -> [1, -1, 0, -3, 0, 3]

gpt: <think> Okay, let's see. The problem is to find the polynomial of the lowest degree that generates a given sequence of numbers. The input is the first m terms, starting from n=0 up to n=m-1. And the output should be the coefficients of that polynomial.

Hmm, right. So how do I approach this? Well, I remember something about finite differences. Maybe the method involves calculating those and finding when they stabilize?

Right! The idea is that for a polynomial of degree k, the (k+1)th differences will be zero. So to find the minimal degree, we can keep taking differences until we get a constant (or zero) sequence. The number of times we take differences before that happens gives us the degree. For example, if the first differences are constant, it's linear; second differences constant gives quadratic, etc.

Wait, but once we know the degree, how do we actually find the coefficients? Oh right, maybe Newton's Forward Difference Formula? But maybe there's another way, like constructing a system of equations and solving them.

Alternatively, there's the method of using the method of finite differences to build the coefficients directly. Here's a thought: Let's say the given sequence is [a0, a1, ..., an-1], then the coefficients of the polynomial can be determined by the first element of each row of the difference table, multiplied by binomial coefficients.

Alternatively, another way is to construct a Vandermonde matrix and solve the linear system. But that might be computationally heavy, especially for higher degrees. Since the points are n=0, 1,...m-1, the Vandermonde would be of size m x (k+1), but since we don't know k initially, we need to increase up until we find the minimal degree. Wait but how?

Alternatively, the method using successive differences can help. Let me think. The coefficients can be calculated using a kind of iterative approach based on the initial terms of the difference tables. For example, Newton's formula uses divided differences, but since the points here are consecutive integers, maybe there's a simpler approach.

Alternatively, perhaps building the coefficients step by step. Let me see. Suppose the terms are given as f(0), f(1), f(2),..., f(m-1). The minimal polynomial will have degree d, so we can form a system of equations. To avoid having to handle linear algebra manually, maybe there's a smarter way.

# APL (Dyalog Unicode), 29 bytes

Try it online!

This one leverages the library operator roman that does various arithmetic over roman numerals (that is, the input is also a roman numeral). So this basically computes the 1-based range {⍳⍵} over roman numerals with the input value 'C' (100), appends a space to each string ,∘' '¨ and flattens them ∊.

String[] t=",X,XX,XXX,XL,L,LX,LXX,LXXX,XC,,I,II,III,IV,V,VI,VII,VIII,IX".split(",");
//Create an array of numerals, first half represents tens place, second half represents ones place
for(int i=1;i<100;i++){
//Loop 99 times
a+=t[i/10]+t[i%10+10]+" ";
//Add tens place and ones place to the string
}return a+"C";
//Add numeral for 100 and return the string

# TeX, 354 bytes

\let~\let~\d\def~\a\advance~\b\divide~\x\expandafter~\f\ifnum{~~\newcount~\n~\i~\j~\k~\u~\v}~~\or\d\p#1{\ifcase#1C~2~L~5~X~2~V~5~I\fi}\d\q#1{\p{#1~}}\d\r{\j0
\v100\d\m{\d\w{\f\n<\v\else\p\j\a\n-\v\x\w\fi}\w\f\n>0\k\j\u\v\d\g{\a\k2\b\u\q\k}\g\f\q\k=2\g\fi\a\n\u\f\n<\v\a\n-\u\a\j2\b\v\q\j\else\p\k\fi\x\m\fi}\m}\i1\d\c{
\f\i<101 \n\i\r\a\i1 \x\c\fi}\c\bye


Some explanation: TeX provides a built-in command \romannumeral for converting numbers to Roman numerals. As the question doesn't allow to use built-in functions, the above code is a golfed version of the same algorithm Knuth's original TeX compiler uses for \romannumeral (see TeX: The Program, § 69, print_roman_int) re-implemented in TeX.

As he wants to leave the joy of puzzling out how this code works to the reader, Knuth refuses to give an explanation of this part of the code. So I'll follow suit and just give an ungolfed and slightly modified version, which is closer to the original than the above code:

\newcount\n
\newcount\j
\newcount\k
\newcount\u
\newcount\v

\def\chrnum#1{\ifcase#1m\or 2\or d\or 5\or c\or 2\or l\or 5\or x\or 2\or v\or 5\or i\fi}
\def\chrnumM#1{\chrnum{#1\or}}

\def\roman#1{%
\n=#1\relax
\j=0\relax
\v=1000\relax
\def\mainloop{%
\def\while{%
\ifnum\n<\v
\else
\chrnum\j
\advance\n -\v
\expandafter\while
\fi
}\while
\ifnum\n>0\relax
\k=\j \advance\k 2\relax
\u=\v \divide\u \chrnumM\k
\ifnum\chrnumM\k=2\relax
\advance\k 2\relax
\divide\u \chrnumM\k
\fi
\advance\n \u
\ifnum\n<\v
\advance\n -\u
\advance\j 2\relax
\divide\v \chrnumM\j
\else
\chrnum\k
\fi
\expandafter\mainloop
\fi
}\mainloop
}

\newcount\i \i=1
\def\countloop{%
\ifnum\i<100\relax
\roman\i\
\advance\i 1
\expandafter\countloop
\fi
}\countloop
\bye<#sep#>-

#include <bits/stdc++.h>
using namespace std;

typedef long long ll;
const int MOD = 1e9 + 7;
const int MAXN = 2000 + 5;
const int GRID_SIZE = 1001;

int main() {
    ios_base::sync_with_stdio(false);
    cin.tie(0);

    int N, M, R;
    cin >> N >> M >> R;

    vector<int> X(N), Y(N), B(N);
    vector grid(GRID_SIZE, vector<int>(GRID_SIZE, 0));
    for (int i = 0; i < N; ++i) {
        cin >> X[i] >> Y[i] >> B[i];
        grid[X[i]][Y[i]] = 1;
    }

    // Compute prefix sums
    vector prefix(GRID_SIZE, vector<ll>(GRID_SIZE, 0));
    for (int i = 1; i < GRID_SIZE; ++i) {
        for (int j = 1; j < GRID_SIZE; ++j) {
            prefix[i][j] = grid[i][j] + prefix[i - 1][j] + prefix[i][j - 1] - prefix[i - 1][j - 1];
        }
    }

    auto query = [&](int x1, int y1, int x2, int y2) {
        x1 = max(1, x1);
        y1 = max(1, y1);
        x2 = min(GRID_SIZE - 1, x2);
        y2 = min(GRID_SIZE - 1, y2);
        if (x1 > x2 || y1 > y2) return 0LL;
        return prefix[x2][y2] - prefix[x1 - 1][y2] - prefix[x2][y1 - 1] + prefix[x1 - 1][y1 - 1];
    };

    // Precompute combinations
    vector comb(N + 1, vector<ll>(N + 1, 0));
    comb[0][0] = 1;
    for (int a = 1; a <= N; ++a) {
        comb[a][0] = 1;
        comb[a][a] = 1;
        for (int b = 1; b < a; ++b) {
            comb[a][b] = (comb[a - 1][b - 1] + comb[a - 1][b]) % MOD;
        }
    }

    vector<int> T(N);
    for (int i = 0; i < N; ++i) {
        int x = X[i], y = Y[i];
        int x1 = x - R, x2 = x + R;
        int y1 = y - R, y2 = y + R;
        T[i] = query(x1, y1, x2, y2);
    }

    vector A(N, 0LL);
    for (int i = 0; i < N; ++i) {
        int t = T[i];
        int rem = N - t;
        ll c = rem >= 0 ? comb[rem][M] : 0;
        A[i] = (comb[N][M] - c + MOD) % MOD;
    }

    ll sum_single = 0;
    for (int i = 0; i < N; ++i) {
        sum_single = (sum_single + 1LL * B[i] * B[i] % MOD * A[i]) % MOD;
    }

    ll sum_pairs = 0;
    for (int i = 0; i < N; ++i) {
        for (int j = i + 1; j < N; ++j) {
            int x1_i = X[i] - R, x2_i = X[i] + R;
            int y1_i = Y[i] - R, y2_i = Y[i] + R;
            int x1_j = X[j] - R, x2_j = X[j] + R;
            int y1_j = Y[j] - R, y2_j = Y[j] + R;

            int x1 = max(x1_i, x1_j);
            int x2 = min(x2_i, x2_j);
            int y1 = max(y1_i, y1_j);
            int y2 = min(y2_i, y2_j);

            int intersect = query(x1, y1, x2, y2);
            int a = T[i], b = T[j];
            int d = a + b - intersect;

            ll term = (comb[N][M] - comb[N - a][M]) % MOD;
            term = (term - comb[N - b][M] + MOD) % MOD;
            term = (term + comb[N - d][M]) % MOD;
            term = (term + MOD) % MOD;

            sum_pairs = (sum_pairs + (1LL * B[i] * B[j] % MOD) * term) % MOD;
        }
    }
    sum_pairs = (sum_pairs * 2) % MOD;

    ll total = (sum_single + sum_pairs) % MOD;
    cout << total << endl;

    return 0;

Batteries, Chargers, and Battery Management Systems.

Location: Oxford, England

80% charge in one hour?

The only way I can get an 80% charge into a Li battery is if I start with the battery fully discharged or near enough. Within +5/0 minutes (it's never faster than 60 minutes) I can hit 80% which presupposes I've towed the car to a charger as there isn't a BMS car system around that will allow a battery to be discharged completely. With a 10% residual charge, although you can reach 80% in less than an hour, you will not reach 90% for 90 minutes or so. This means that under any normal condition you can not put 80% into a battery within an hour.

The problem is that irrespective of what value of constant current I start with, as soon as the voltage reaches 4.2 the current rapidly drops off. You can move that transition point backwards or forwards but what you gain in one charging phase, you lose in another.

The batteries I tested were Samsung 30Q & 35E, LG MJ1; all of which performed as per their data sheets although an 80% charge becomes a difficult to achieve target the more cycles you put it through. The 35E is alone in having a maximum charge current considerably less than 1C.

I ask this because here in Europe many governments, even down to the lowest parish council, are wanting to ban combustion engines in 10 or 20 years time and in the 10 years that I've been involved in this, the rate of technical progress (as measured in energy capacity) has been miserable. All good virtue signalling to the voters but it isn't going to end well at this rate.

izeman   10 GW

Location: vienna, austria

Re: 80% charge in one hour?

The only question i see is in the topic
So what IS your question? If it is possible to charge a battery to 80% in less than an hour.
Simple answer: Yes. It just depends on the chemistry if they will survive it or not. I got graphene rc-lipo that can be charged @4C. So it's full in 15min.
You write that the charge current gets less very quickly as soon as the battery reaches 4.2V. Sure. It's full by then. Charging happens as CC/CV. Which means that in the beginning you see a Constant Current, and as soon as the charger's output voltage and the battery's voltage start to match (near the end of charge) the current will drop (even to zero amps).

Joined: Dec 18 2018 2:06pm

Re: 80% charge in one hour?

The key is defining YOUR version of 100%, not going by the absolute top voltage possible according to the batt maker who wants a short cycle life.

Drop your Ah expectation a little, back the finish voltage down a lttle.

Peace of mind, fast charge now possible, bank lasts much longer and really not giving up much range.

Re: 80% charge in one hour?

The higher the discharge capabilities of a given lithium battery, the faster it can be charged. Monitor the heat, and slowly raise the amps you are charging with. As the cell gets closer to being full, you must lower the charging amps. Again, continuously monitor the heat...

Re: 80% charge in one hour?

Thats only partly true.
Each Lithium chemistry has its own parameter for fast discharge and fast charge to yield a reasonable cycle life.
Watch in the specification of your buy. and only charge fast when needed.

Re: 80% charge in one hour?

Feb 14 2019 7:46am
The only way I can get an 80% charge into a Li battery is if I start with the battery fully discharged or near enough.
Sounds like you are saying that if you start with a battery charged to 50% you can't get another 80% worth of charge in it. Yes, that's true - but sorta obvious.
With a 10% residual charge, although you can reach 80% in less than an hour, you will not reach 90% for 90 minutes or so. This means that under any normal condition you can not put 80% into a battery within an hour.
See above.
The problem is that irrespective of what value of constant current I start with, as soon as the voltage reaches 4.2 the current rapidly drops off.
Yes. That's how lithium ion batteries charge - CC/CV.
You can move that transition point backwards or forwards but what you gain in one charging phase, you lose in another.
No. If you charge at a higher C rate you do not "lose" the ability to charge in the CV section. You just can't accelerate the CV section any faster than voltage allows.

Re: 80% charge in one hour?

Well, you can accelerate (or get around) the CV charging section especially if your termination is significantly under 4.<#sep#>-

#include <vector>
#include <string>
#include <climits>

using namespace std;

struct Config {
    bool is_row;
    pair<char, char> even_p;
    pair<char, char> odd_p;
    int even_start;
    int odd_start;
    int cost;
};

vector<pair<pair<char, char>, pair<char, char>>> pairings = {
    {{'A','G'}, {'C','T'}},
    {{'A','C'}, {'G','T'}},
    {{'A','T'}, {'C','G'}}
};

int compute_row_cost(const vector<string>& grid, const pair<char, char>& even_p, const pair<char, char>& odd_p, int even_start, int odd_start) {
    int cost = 0;
    int n = grid.size();
    int m = grid[0].size();
    for (int i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
        for (int j = 0; j < m; ++j) {
            char expected;
            if (i % 2 == 0) {
                if (j % 2 == 0) {
                    expected = even_p.first;
                } else {
                    expected = even_p.second;
                }
            } else {
                if (j % 2 == 0) {
                    expected = odd_p.first;
                } else {
                    expected = odd_p.second;
                }
            }
            if (expected != grid[i][j]) cost++;
        }
    }
    return cost;
}

int compute_col_cost(const vector<string>& grid, const pair<char, char>& even_p, const pair<char, char>& odd_p, int even_start, int odd_start) {
    int cost = 0;
    int n = grid.size();
    int m = grid[0].size();
    for (int i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
        for (int j = 0; j < m; ++j) {
            char expected;
            if (j % 2 == 0) {
                if (i % 2 == 0) {
                    expected = even_p.first;
                } else {
                    expected = even_p.second;
                }
            } else {
                if (i % 2 == 0) {
                    expected = odd_p.first;
                } else {
                    expected = odd_p.second;
                }
            }
            if (expected != grid[i][j]) cost++;
        }
    }
    return cost;
}

Config find_best_config(const vector<string>& grid) {
    Config best;
    best.cost = INT_MAX;

    // Check row-wise candidates
    for (const auto& p : pairings) {
        const auto& p1 = p.first;
        const auto& p2 = p.second;
        for (int even_rev = 0; even_rev < 2; ++even_rev) {
            auto even_p = (even_rev == 0) ? p1 : p2;
            auto odd_p = (even_rev == 0) ? p2 : p1;
            for (int even_start = 0; even_start < 2; ++even_start) {
                pair<char, char> even_pair = { even_p[even_start], even_p[1 - even_start] };
                for (int odd_start = 0; odd_start < 2; ++odd_start) {
                    pair<char, char> odd_pair = { odd_p[odd_start], odd_p[1 - odd_start] };
                    int cost = compute_row_cost(grid, even_pair, odd_pair, even_start, odd_start);
                    if (cost < best.cost) {
                        best = { true, even_pair, odd_pair, even_start, odd_start, cost };
                    }
                }
            }
        }
    }

    // Check column-wise candidates
    for (const auto& p : pairings) {
        const auto& p1 = p.first;
        const auto& p2 = p.second;
        for (int even_rev = 0; even_rev < 2; ++even_rev) {
            auto even_p = (even_rev == 0) ? p1 : p2;
            auto odd_p = (even_rev == 0) ? p2 : p1;
            for (int even_start = 0; even_start < 2; ++even_start) {
                pair<char, char> even_pair = { even_p[even_start], even_p[1 - even_start] };
                for (int odd_start = 0; odd_start < 2; ++odd_start) {
                    pair<char, char> odd_pair = { odd_p[odd_start], odd_p[1 - odd_start] };
                    int cost = compute_col_cost(grid, even_pair, odd_pair, even_start, odd_start);
                    if (cost < best.cost) {
                        best = { false, even_pair, odd_pair, even_start, odd_start, cost };
                    }
                }
            }
        }
    }

    return best;
}

vector<string> generate_grid(const Config& config, int n, int m) {
    vector<string> res(n, string(m, ' '));
    if (config.is_row) {
        for (int i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
            for (int j = 0; j < m; ++j) {
                if (i % 2 == 0) {
                    res[i][j] = (j % 2 == 0) ? config.even_p.first : config.even_p.second;
                } else {
                    res[i][j] = (j % 2 == 0) ? config.odd_p.first : config.odd_p.second;
                }
            }
        }
    } else {
        for (int i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
            for (int j = 0; j < m; ++j) {
                if (j % 2 == 0) {
                    res[i][j] = (i % 2 == 0) ? config.even_p.first : config.even_p.second;
                } else {
                    res[i][j] = (i % 2 == 0) ? config.odd_p.first : config.odd_p.second;
                }
            }
        }
    }
    return res;
}

int main() {
    int n, m;
    scanf("%d %d", &n, &m);
    vector<string> grid(n);
    for (int i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
        char buf[300001];
        scanf("%s", buf);
        grid[i] = buf;
    }

    Config best = find_best_config(grid);
    vector<string> output = generate_grid(best, n, m);

    for (const string& row : output) {
        printf("%s\n", row.c_str());
    }

    return 0;

Wait, let's think of the problem in terms of sequences. We need a sequence where:

1. No two consecutive elements are the same.
2. There does not exist indices a < b < c < d such that color(a) = color(c) and color(b) = color(d), and color(a) ≠ color(b).

Let me rephrase condition 2: there are no two pairs of equal colors, with the first pair (a, c) and the second pair (b, d), such that the colors of these pairs are different and the indices are ordered a < b < c < d.

This seems similar to avoiding a configuration where two different colors alternate with each other with two steps in between. For example, if you have color X at positions a and c, and color Y at positions b and d, with a < b < c < d, then this forms an alternating pattern X, Y, X, Y spread out over the days. So, the forbidden pattern is an alternation of two different colors with two days in between each occurrence. Hence, the problem is to avoid such a "spread out" alternation.

In other words, the sequence cannot contain two different colors that each repeat at least twice with the other color in between. So, if a color X appears multiple times, any other color Y cannot appear between two Xs and then have another Y after the second X. Wait, more precisely, for any two different colors X and Y, we cannot have a situation where X is followed by Y, followed by X, followed by Y, even if there are other colors in between. But in our problem, the indices a < b < c < d can have any other elements in between; they don't have to be consecutive.

Therefore, the forbidden pattern is a subsequence of four elements in the order X, Y, X, Y where X ≠ Y.

Ah, yes, that's a key observation. The forbidden configuration is equivalent to having a subsequence that alternates between two different colors, regardless of what's in between. So, even if there are other colors in between the X and Y, as long as there exists four days where the colors form the alternation X, Y, X, Y (in order), the sequence is invalid.

Therefore, the problem reduces to finding the maximum length of a sequence over n symbols where:

1. No two consecutive symbols are the same.
2. There is no alternating subsequence of length 4 using two different symbols.

This is similar to the concept of avoiding the abelian square or other forbidden subsequences. In combinatorics, the maximum length of such a sequence is known to be quadratic in terms of n. Wait, but I might be misremembering.

Alternatively, this problem is equivalent to asking for the maximum length of a sequence that is 2-sparse (no two consecutive same elements) and does not contain an alternating subsequence of length 4. The question is then to find the maximum length in terms of n.

Re: 80% charge in one hour?

Well, you can accelerate (or get around) the CV charging section especially if your termination is significantly under 4.2. You do it by understanding the charge profile, shooting past your CV section in CC mode by setting a higher terminating voltage than normal, and terminating manually or by power transferred around the point where the resting voltage will be what you want.

Joined: Dec 18 2018 2:06pm

Re: 80% charge in one hour?

LI of any chemistry will **accept** (pull, demand) a rate higher than is good for it wrt longevity.

Up to you what you want the max rate to be allowed, "made available" by the charge source.

From the batt health POV, no Absorb / CV stage at all is needed, can just charge **to** a termination voltage and stop.

The Bulk/CC to Absorb/CV transition will happen at a much higher/later SoC% with low current than with a high rate.

You can maintain a bit higher charging voltage safely with a high charge rate, just means the settling to a lower resting voltage will be a greater drop, and SoC will not be as high

compared to low currents say overnight.

You do not actually want to get LI to **maximum** Full in normal cycling, unless you really need every last bit of range/Ah capacity for your use case.

Lower volts, lower amps and stopping sooner are all conducive to greater longevity.

I think I understand the question. Some cars with liquid cooling are now pulling up to 1.7C for a small portion of the charge cycle. None are doing a full charge in an hour. However, some can definitely do 80% in under an hour, as seen in this video.

We have been driving a Chevy Bolt for 19 months, and while the fast charge rate is an annoyance when on trips beyond its 200+ mile range, this is not a big deal.

The real issues are lack of fast charging infrastructure, and the high cost of batteries.

Both of these problems can be solved a lot easier than many of the other challenges of dealing with climate change, over population, resource depletion, etc. I don't think politicians, or most of us, can get our heads around the exponential nature of our problem.

#include <vector>
#include <queue>
#include <iostream>
#include <tuple>
#include <algorithm>
using namespace std;

pair<int, vector<int>> solve(const vector<vector<pair<int, char>>>& adj, int n, char target) {
    vector<bool> visited(n+1, false);
    vector<int> x(n+1, -1);
    vector<int> flips;
    bool possible = true;

    for (int u = 1; u <= n; ++u) {
        if (!visited[u]) {
            queue<int> q;
            q.push(u);
            visited[u] = true;
            x[u] = 0;
            vector<int> component;
            component.push_back(u);
            int cnt_ones = 0;

            while (!q.empty()) {
                int current = q.front();
                q.pop();
                cnt_ones += x[current];

                for (auto [v, c] : adj[current]) {
                    bool t_i = (c != target);
                    int desired = x[current] ^ t_i;

                    if (!visited[v]) {
                        visited[v] = true;
                        x[v] = desired;
                        q.push(v);
                        component.push_back(v);
                    } else {
                        if (x[v] != desired) {
                            possible = false;
                            while (!q.empty()) q.pop();
                            goto end_component;
                        }
                    }
                }
            }

            end_component:
            if (!possible) break;

            int m = min(cnt_ones, (int)component.size() - cnt_ones);
            if (cnt_ones <= (int)component.size() - cnt_ones) {
                for (int node : component) {
                    if (x[node] == 1) {
                        flips.push_back(node);
                    }
                }
            } else {
                for (int node : component) {
                    if (x[node] == 0) {
                        flips.push_back(node);
                    }
                }
            }
        }
    }

    if (possible) {
        return {flips.size(), flips};
    } else {
        return {-1, {}};
    }
}

int main() {
    ios_base::sync_with_stdio(false);
    cin.tie(nullptr);

    int n, m;
    cin >> n >> m;

    vector<vector<pair<int, char>>> adj(n+1);
    for (int i = 0; i < m; ++i) {
        int u, v;
        char c;
        cin >> u >> v >> c;
        adj[u].emplace_back(v, c);
        adj[v].emplace_back(u, c);
    }

    auto res_r = solve(adj, n, 'R');
    auto res_b = solve(adj, n, 'B');

    int best = -1;
    vector<int> answer;

    if (res_r.first != -1) {
        best = res_r.first;
        answer = res_r.second;
    }
    if (res_b.first != -1) {
        if (best == -1 || res_b.first < best) {
            best = res_b.first;
            answer = res_b.second;
        }
    }

    if (best == -1) {
        cout << -1 << '\n';
    } else {
        cout << best << '\n';
        for (size_t i = 0; i < answer.size(); ++i) {
            if (i > 0) cout << ' ';
            cout << answer[i];
        }
        cout << '\n';
    }

    return 0;

The problem resembles the concept of extremal graph theory, where we want the maximum number of edges in a graph without a certain subgraph. Here, it's a sequence with maximum length without a certain subpattern. The forbidden pattern is a 4-length alternation of two different colors. Such problems often have bounds related to n^2 or similar.

Wait, in 1959, Erdos and Szekeres showed that any sequence of more than (k-1)(l-1) elements contains either an increasing subsequence of length k or a decreasing subsequence of length l. But that's for permutations.

Alternatively, for forbidden Davenport-Schinzel sequences, which avoid alternating subsequences. Specifically, a Davenport-Schinzel sequence of order s is a sequence that does not contain any alternating subsequences of length s+1. For s=3, this would mean avoiding any subsequence of the form a, b, a, b. Which is exactly our forbidden pattern! So, Davenport-Schinzel sequences of order 3 avoid any alternating subsequence (not necessarily consecutive) of two different symbols. Therefore, our problem is equivalent to finding the maximum length of a Davenport-Schinzel sequence of order 3 over n symbols, with the additional constraint that no two consecutive symbols are the same. But wait, Davenport-Schinzel sequences already require that no two consecutive symbols are the same. Yes, because in Davenport-Schinzel sequences, repetitions of the same symbol must be separated by at least one different symbol. But our problem has the same constraint (no two consecutive days with the same color) and the additional constraint of avoiding the alternation of two different symbols in a subsequence of length 4. Therefore, our problem is exactly the Davenport-Schinzel sequence of order 3. Thus, the maximum length is given by the Davenport-Schinzel constant for order 3, which is known to be n*2^(alpha(n)) ), but for practical purposes, it's nearly linear. Wait, no, for Davenport-Schinzel sequences of order s, the maximum length for s=3 is lambda_3(n), which is known to be Θ(n log n). However, I might be misremembering. Wait, actually, Davenport-Schinzel sequences of order 2 have maximum length n(n-1)/2 +1, but with order 3, the maximum length is O(n alpha(n)), where alpha(n) is the inverse Ackermann function. Once the degree is found, then next step is to compute the standard coefficients.

So once we have the degree d, the minimal one can be found via the first (d+1) terms, because a polynomial of degree d is uniquely determined by d+1 points. 

So to compute the coefficients of degree d, we need to build a linear system with d+1 terms. For example, the given sequence has m terms, but we can take the first d+1 terms to uniquely determine the polynomial. Because with more terms than required, but the problem specifies that the polynomial that fits all terms is possible. 

Alternatively, take all the given terms except the first d+1, but the problem says "the polynomial of lowest degree that could have generated the input sequence", so those terms must all fit. 

Thus, when solving for the coefficients, the system must be exactly rank (d+1), and so the first d+1 terms are sufficient to find the coefficients.

But to handle it in code, perhaps simply building a system with the first d+1 terms and solving it would be sufficient. Since with the minimal degree, the coefficients will be unique.

Wait but solving the system requires setting up a linear system. Let's see:

Suppose we have to find the coefficients a_0, a_1,...a_d such that:

for i from 0 to d:

   a_0 + a_1 * i + a_2 * i^2 ... a_d *i^d = s[i]

Then the set of equations is:

equation 0: a0 = s[0]

equation 1: a0 + a1*1 +a2*1^2 + ... + a_d*1^d = s[1]

...

equation d: a0 + a1*d + ... +a_d*d^d = s[d]

We can set up the matrix and vector, then solve the linear equations.

For example, for degree 1 and terms up to i=1:

The matrix:

[1, 0 ] → equation 0, for n=0: a0 = s[0]

[1,1] → equation 1<#sep#>Prioitise self-generation bug
### Describe the bug
When running CLOVER, there are two possible flows for how the code selects its source of power, which are set within the `scenario_inputs.yaml` file:
- `prioiritise_self_generation: true`, where CLOVER should attempt to get power from the solar and storage first,
- and `prioritise_self_generation: false`, where CLOVER should get its power from the grid first.

However, when running a CLOVER `optimisation`, there is **no difference** between these two flows, with the same outputs being selected despite the second scenario being expected to produce systems with a lower renewable capacity due to the reliance on the grid as the primary source of power.

### To Reproduce
Steps to reproduce the behavior:
1. Set up two identical scenarios in the `scenario_inputs.yaml` file with the only difference between the two being the `prioritise_self_generation` flag;
2. Set up two identical optimisations in the `optimisation_inputs.yaml` file with the only difference between the two being the scenario that each uses;
3. Run an optimisation with `python -u -m src.clover --location <location_name> --optimisation`;
4. See error.

### Expected behaviour
The two scenario should produce different results, with the `true` scenario selecting a higher renewable capacity and the `false` scenario selecting a smaller renewable capacity.

### Screenshots
Screenshots are not needed to explain this problem.

## Additional Information
### Desktop
**(please complete the following information)**
 - OS: Linux, applies to all
 - Installation setup: downloaded source from GitHub
 - Version: 5.0.1b1
 - Branch: `dev`

### Modifications
This version is up-to-date with `origin/dev`.

     battery_storage_profile.columns = pd.Index([ColumnHeader.STORAGE_PROFILE.value])
     grid_energy.columns = pd.Index([ColumnHeader.GRID_ENERGY.value])

Re: 80% charge in one hour?

Feb 15 2019 7:21pm
The actual capacity of that 75kw battery will be greater by about 10% or even more.
To be more accurate, the car will only let you use about 90% of that 75kw. It behooves manufacturers to advertise the highest capacity battery they can, so they do. Software then prevents 100% discharge.
My criticism of the EV manufacturers is that the claims that are made about charging are fanciful. We have politicians who believe that if they say something often enough, eg affordable and with charging infrastructure, then it happens as if by magic and anyone who makes them isn’t going to disagree.
My first EV was a Leaf. The 80% claim was certainly accurate when the car was new. Since it was a 24kwhr pack, a 50kw charger could get it to 80% in about half an hour. After five years ESR increased and the car could barely reach 80% in an hour.
The observation by bill von that a 335 mile battery is progress isn’t surely due to any technological breakthrough but simply by packing more batteries in? You can only go so far with that before the weight of the battery begins to impact the performance of the vehicle.
Exactly.

That 335 mile range is, of course, to EV manufacturers packing lots of batteries in. If they did not have a high energy density battery to start with the weight would make the design impossible.

I used one of the first 18650 batteries in a satellite phone I designed back around 2000. It was 1300mah and was the best we could get at that point. Now the same size/weight cell is more than twice that - due to slow, incremental improvements in cell technology.
--bill von
