#include <bits/stdc++.h>
using namespace std;

int main() {
    string s;
    cin >> s;
    int n = s.size();
    vector<unordered_set<char>> allowed(n);
    for (int i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
        allowed[i] = {'a','b','c','d','e','f'};
    }
    int m;
    cin >> m;
    for (int k = 0; k < m; ++k) {
        int pos;
        string chars;
        cin >> pos >> chars;
        pos--;
        allowed[pos].clear();
        for (char c : chars) {
            allowed[pos].insert(c);
        }
    }
    vector<int> original_counts(6, 0);
    for (char c : s) {
        original_counts[c - 'a']++;
    }
    vector<vector<int>> prefix(6, vector<int>(n, 0));
    for (int d = 0; d < 6; ++d) {
        char c = 'a' + d;
        int sum = 0;
        for (int i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
            if (allowed[i].count(c)) {
                sum++;
            }
            prefix[d][i] = sum;
        }
    }
    vector<int> available_initial(6);
    for (int d = 0; d < 6; ++d) {
        available_initial[d] = prefix[d][n-1];
    }
    bool possible = true;
    for (int d = 0; d < 6; ++d) {
        if (available_initial[d] < original_counts[d]) {
            possible = false;
            break;
        }
    }
    if (!possible) {
        cout << "Impossible" << endl;
        return 0;
    }
    vector<int> current_used(6, 0);
    string result;
    for (int i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
        vector<char> candidates;
        for (char c : {'a','b','c','d','e','f'}) {
            if (allowed[i].count(c)) {
                candidates.push_back(c);
            }
        }
        sort(candidates.begin(), candidates.end());
        bool found = false;
        for (char c : candidates) {
            int d = c - 'a';
            if (current_used[d] >= original_counts[d]) continue;
            bool ok = true;
            for (int d_check = 0; d_check < 6; ++d_check) {
                int required = original_counts[d_check] - current_used[d_check];
                if (d_check == d) required--;
                int available_remaining = available_initial[d_check] - prefix[d_check][i];
                if (available_remaining < required) {
                    ok = false;
                    break;
                }
            }
            if (ok) {
                current_used[d]++;
                result += c;
                found = true;
                break;
            }
        }
        if (!found) {
            cout << "Impossible" << endl;
            return 0;
        }
    }
    cout << result << endl;
    return 0;

equation 1: a0 + a1*1 +a2*1^2 + ... + a_d*1^d = s[1]

...

equation d: a0 + a1*d + ... +a_d*d^d = s[d]

We can set up the matrix and vector, then solve the linear equations.

For example, for degree 1 and terms up to i=1:

The matrix:

[1, 0 ] → equation 0, for n=0: a0 = s[0]

[1,1] → equation 1 for n=1: a0 + a1 = s[1]

This matrix inversion gives [s[0], s[1] -s[0] ].

Another example with degree 2:

the matrix is:

row 0 (i=0): 1 0 0 → equation a0 = S[0]

row 1 (i=1):1,1,1 → a0 + a1 + a2 = S[1]

row 2 (i=2): 1,2,4 → a0 + 2a1 +4a2 = S[2]

The solution would be found via matrix inversion.

Thus, solving this system is the way to go once degree is known.

Now, how to do this in code? So the steps would be:

1. Determine the degree d of the minimal polynomial.

2. Take the first d+1 terms of the input (since d+1 points are enough)

3. Set up the system of equations for those points, and solve for coefficients a_0 to a_d.

But setting up the linear equations and solving them in code.

Alternatively, in code, the system can be represented as a matrix and a vector, then solved using Gaussian elimination or some method.

But implementing Gaussian elimination might be a bit tedious. Hmm.

Alternatively, for even better code golfing, one might find a way to compute the coefficients directly via the difference table. Because sometimes the coefficients can be computed as the first entries of the differences divided by certain things, but how?

Let me think again about the binomial coefficients approach.

The formula derived in Newton's forward difference formula is that the polynomial can be expressed as:

f(n) = c0 + c1*n + c2*n(n-1)/2! + c3*n(n-1)(n-2)/3! + ... 

Where the c's are the leading terms from the difference tables. So the first coefficients are the terms from the zeroth level (c0), the next is the first difference's first term, then the second difference's first term, etc.

Thus, in the example of [0,1,4,9], the coefficients c0 =0, c1 is 1 (the first difference's first entry), c2 is 2 (the second difference's first entry). The formula then is 0 +1*n +2*(n(n-1)/2) → expanding this gives 0 +n + (n²-n) = n².

Thus the standard coefficient is 1 for n², 0 for n and 0 for the constant.

Wait so the coefficients in standard form can be found by expanding the combination terms.

To compute the standard coefficients, we can compute the binomial expansion coefficients.

So the process would be:

The coefficients in the Newton expansion (they form the coefficients c0, c1, ..., cd from the difference table) are first entries of each difference level. 

Thus for the minimal degree, the leading terms (first elements of each difference level's array) are the coefficients for each term in the binomial expression.

So the standard form's coefficients can be derived by expanding each binomial term and accumulating the coefficients for each power of n.

Thus, this method may be more efficient and easier to calculate.

So, here's a plan:

1. Find the degree d via difference steps as described earlier.

2. Collect the first elements of each difference level up to d. Those are c0, c1, c2, ..., cd.

3. Convert these into the standard polynomial coefficients by expanding the binomial coefficient terms.

The expansion requires for each term ck (starts at 0), we have a term ck multiplied by the binomial coefficient (n choose k). 

The standard form's coefficients can be found by expressing each binomial term as a sum of monomials up to degree k, then adding all the terms together.

For instance, the binomial term for k is c_k * C(n, k) = c_k / k! * n*(n-1)...(n-k+1).

Which when expanded, is c_k /k! [n^k - ... terms of lower degree].

Thus, each term contributes to various coefficients from degree 0 to k. 

Therefore, to calculate all standard coefficients up to degree d:

We can represent each binomial term as a list of coefficients, and add them all together.

Let me see:

The c0 is multiplied by C(n,0) =1 → so contributes just a0 = c0.

The c1 * C(n,1) = c1 * n → contributes 0 to the constant term, c1 to the linear coefficient, etc.

The c2 * C(n, 2) = c2*(n(n-1)/2!) → expand this to (c2/2) n^2 - (c2/2)n → so contributes to the quadratic term + to the linear term.

Therefore, adding all these terms for the binomial expansion gives the coefficients.

Thus, the steps are:

1. The coefficients c0, c1,...cd are obtained.

2. For each term c_k:

   Multiply c_k by each possible term in C(n,k) expansion, then add to the respective coefficients.

3. The final coefficients are the sum for each degree.

Thus, to compute the coefficients, we can make a list of length d+1 (since degree d), initializing all to zero. For each k from 0 to d:

   The term is c_k multiplied by the binomial coefficients of (n choose k).

   binomial coefficients for n^k term: c_k / k!.

Wait, perhaps a better way to think is:

The expansion of C(n,k) is equal to:

n^k /k! (Terms plus lower degree terms). Not sure.

For example, the monomial C(n, k) can be written as:

C(n,k) = (n/(1))*(n-1)/(2) * ... (n-k+1)/(k) )

But to get it as a polynomial in n, it's:

The coefficients of each exponent can be determined. For example:

C(n,0) = 1 → [1]

C(n,1) = n → coefficients [0,1]

C(n,2) = (n(n-1))/2 → (n^2 –n)/2 → coefficients [0, -0.5, 0.5]

Thus, adding the contributions from each term multiplied by the c_k:

Total polynomial is sum_{k=0}^{d} c_k * (C(n, k) expansion).

So for each k, we can compute the contribution of the term (c_k multiplied by the coefficients of C(n,k)) and add that to the total coefficients array.

   current_term = coefficient c_k

   Wait the coefficients are fractions?

Ah right, the problem says "You can assume the polynomial you are looking for has integer coefficients."

<#sep#> ratings.

Why should we have (for example) city buses that weigh 30,000 pounds empty? That's normal these days, and it's stupid.

With cars, the presence of a few huge gas guzzlers not only drives demand for more of them, but it makes people regard small, efficient vehicles as impractical or unsafe. If every personal vehicle is only allowed say 4 liters per 100km, then we would see the tradeoffs inherent in using an excessive vehicle. Sure-- have your three row SUV, but you're gonna go slow. The incentive would be to use the smallest and lightest vehicle that works for the job.
This is to express my gratitude to Justin of Grin Technologies for his extraordinary measures to save this forum for the benefit of all.

#include <bits/stdc++.h>

using namespace std;

const int INF = 1e9;

int main() {
    int n;
    string s;
    cin >> n >> s;

    vector<int> v_pos, k_pos, o_pos;
    for (int i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
        if (s[i] == 'V') {
            v_pos.push_back(i);
        } else if (s[i] == 'K') {
            k_pos.push_back(i);
        } else {
            o_pos.push_back(i);
        }
    }

    int total_v = v_pos.size();
    int total_k = k_pos.size();
    int total_o = o_pos.size();

    vector<vector<vector<vector<int>>>> dp(total_v + 1,
        vector<vector<vector<int>>>(total_k + 1,
            vector<vector<int>>(total_o + 1,
                vector<int>(4, INF))));

    dp[0][0][0][0] = 0;

    for (int s = 0; s <= total_v + total_k + total_o; ++s) {
        for (int v = 0; v <= total_v; ++v) {
            for (int k = 0; k <= total_k; ++k) {
                int o = s - v - k;
                if (o < 0 || o > total_o) continue;
                for (int l = 0; l < 4; ++l) {
                    if (dp[v][k][o][l] == INF) continue;

                    if (v < total_v) {
                        int new_v = v + 1;
                        int new_k = k;
                        int new_o = o;
                        int new_l = 1;
                        int pos = v_pos[v];
                        int count_k = lower_bound(k_pos.begin(), k_pos.begin() + k, pos) - k_pos.begin();
                        int count_o = lower_bound(o_pos.begin(), o_pos.begin() + o, pos) - o_pos.begin();
                        int cost = pos - (v + count_k + count_o);
                        if (dp[v][k][o][l] + cost < dp[new_v][new_k][new_o][new_l]) {
                            dp[new_v][new_k][new_o][new_l] = dp[v][k][o][l] + cost;
                        }
                    }

                    if (k < total_k && l != 1) {
                        int new_v = v;
                        int new_k = k + 1;
                        int new_o = o;
                        int new_l = 2;
                        int pos = k_pos[k];
                        int count_v_lb = lower_bound(v_pos.begin(), v_pos.begin() + v, pos) - v_pos.begin();
                        int count_o_lb = lower_bound(o_pos.begin(), o_pos.begin() + o, pos) - o_pos.begin();
                        int cost = pos - (count_v_lb + k + count_o_lb);
                        if (dp[v][k][o][l] + cost < dp[new_v][new_k][new_o][new_l]) {
                            dp[new_v][new_k][new_o][new_l] = dp[v][k][o][l] + cost;
                        }
                    }

                    if (o < total_o) {
                        int new_v = v;
                        int new_k = k;
                        int new_o = o + 1;
                        int new_l = 3;
                        int pos = o_pos[o];
                        int count_v_lb = lower_bound(v_pos.begin(), v_pos.begin() + v, pos) - v_pos.begin();
                        int count_k_lb = lower_bound(k_pos.begin(), k_pos.begin() + k, pos) - k_pos.begin();
                        int cost = pos - (count_v_lb + count_k_lb + o);
                        if (dp[v][k][o][l] + cost < dp[new_v][new_k][new_o][new_l]) {
                            dp[new_v][new_k][new_o][new_l] = dp[v][k][o][l] + cost;
                        }
                    }
                }
            }
        }
    }

    int answer = INF;
    for (int l = 0; l < 4; ++l) {
        answer = min(answer, dp[total_v][total_k][total_o][l]);
    }
    cout << answer << endl;

    return 0;

Alternatively, the answer could be n(n-1) +1, which for n=2 is 3, n=3 is 7, but earlier attempts showed that even for n=3, getting to 5 days is difficult. So, perhaps that's not the case.

Wait, in the Davenport-Schinzel sequences of order 3, the maximum length is asymptotically lambda_3(n) ~ 2n alpha(n), where alpha(n) is the inverse Ackermann function, which grows very slowly. For practical n, this is linear. However, exact values are known for small n. For example, for n=2, lambda_3(2)=4. But in our problem, for n=2, the maximum is 3, so maybe the problem has a different constraint. Wait, maybe Davenport-Schinzel sequences allow repetitions as long as they are not consecutive, but our problem doesn't allow any two consecutive repetitions, which is already part of the Davenport-Schinzel definition. Wait, Davenport-Schinzel sequences of order s do not have two consecutive same elements and do not have any alternating subsequences of length s+1. So, for s=3, they avoid any abab subsequences. Therefore, Davenport-Schinzel sequence of order 3 is exactly what we need here. Hence, the maximum length of the sequence is lambda_3(n). However, the exact value of lambda_3(n) is known to be n*2^{alpha(n)} and it's bounded by O(n*2^{alpha(n)} log alpha(n))). But for the purposes of this problem, which seems like a math competition problem, the answer is likely to be 2n -1. 

Alternatively, maybe the problem is related to the concept of a "zigzag" sequence, and the maximum length is n squared. But that doesn't fit with the small n examples.

Wait, another approach: The problem requires that there are no two indices a < b < c < d with color a = color c and color b = color d, different from each other. Suppose we model this as a graph where each node is a color, and edges represent the color transitions. Then, the forbidden pattern corresponds to a rectangle or a cycle of length 4 in the graph's edge sequence. However, since the sequence is linear, not a graph, this might not directly apply.

Alternatively, think of the colors as vertices and transitions as edges. Then, the forbidden pattern is a path of four edges that forms two back-and-forth edges between two colors. For example, X -> Y -> X -> Y. Such a path would be forbidden. Therefore, the problem reduces to finding the longest trail in a complete graph with n vertices (since the bird can change to any color except the current one) that does not contain a closed trail of the form X-Y-X-Y. However, the question is about open trails, not closed, but the forbidden structure is still a specific path.

In graph terms, this is similar to finding the longest path without a P4 (path of length 3, which has 4 vertices). But the forbidden pattern is not exactly a path, it's two edges between two pairs of nodes in a specific alternating order.

Alternatively, considering that each time we use a color, we have to avoid creating the forbidden quartet with any previous color. So, each time we place a color, we have to make sure that we don't have two previous instances of another color that could form the forbidden pattern.

This seems like a problem that can be modeled with forbidden configurations in hypergraphs, but I don't have enough knowledge to proceed further.

#include <bits/stdc++.h>
using namespace std;

vector<pair<int, int>> edges;
vector<vector<pair<int, int>>> adj;
vector<int> disc, low;
vector<bool> is_bridge;
int time_counter;

void tarjan(int u, int parent_edge) {
    disc[u] = low[u] = ++time_counter;
    for (auto [v, idx] : adj[u]) {
        if (idx == parent_edge) continue;
        if (!disc[v]) {
            tarjan(v, idx);
            low[u] = min(low[u], low[v]);
            if (low[v] > disc[u]) {
                is_bridge[idx] = true;
            }
        } else {
            low[u] = min(low[u], disc[v]);
        }
    }
}

int main() {
    ios_base::sync_with_stdio(false);
    cin.tie(nullptr);

    int n, m;
    cin >> n >> m;

    edges.resize(m);
    adj.resize(n + 1);
    for (int i = 0; i < m; ++i) {
        int u, v;
        cin >> u >> v;
        edges[i] = {u, v};
        adj[u].emplace_back(v, i);
        adj[v].emplace_back(u, i);
    }

    disc.assign(n + 1, 0);
    low.assign(n + 1, 0);
    is_bridge.assign(m, false);
    time_counter = 0;

    for (int u = 1; u <= n; ++u) {
        if (!disc[u]) {
            tarjan(u, -1);
        }
    }

    vector<vector<pair<int, int>>> adj_non_bridges(n + 1);
    for (int i = 0; i < m; ++i) {
        if (!is_bridge[i]) {
            int u = edges[i].first;
            int v = edges[i].second;
            adj_non_bridges[u].emplace_back(v, i);
            adj_non_bridges[v].emplace_back(u, i);
        }
    }

    vector<bool> visited(n + 1, false);
    vector<bool> processed_edge(m, false);
    vector<int> answer;

    for (int u = 1; u <= n; ++u) {
        if (!visited[u]) {
            queue<int> q;
            q.push(u);
            visited[u] = true;
            int node_count = 0;
            vector<int> component_edges;

            while (!q.empty()) {
                int v = q.front();
                q.pop();
                node_count++;

                for (auto [neighbor, idx] : adj_non_bridges[v]) {
                    if (!processed_edge[idx]) {
                        component_edges.push_back(idx);
                        processed_edge[idx] = true;
                    }
                    if (!visited[neighbor]) {
                        visited[neighbor] = true;
                        q.push(neighbor);
                    }
                }
            }

            if (component_edges.size() == node_count) {
                for (int idx : component_edges) {
                    answer.push_back(idx + 1);
                }
            }
        }
    }

    sort(answer.begin(), answer.end());
    cout << answer.size() << '\n';
    for (size_t i = 0; i < answer.size(); ++i) {
        if (i > 0) cout << ' ';
        cout << answer[i];
    }
    cout << '\n';

</think>To solve the problem of determining the maximum number of days Rainbow can live with \( n \) colors, while ensuring no two consecutive days have the same color and avoiding a specific forbidden pattern, we can use the concept of Davenport-Schinzel sequences.

### Problem Recap:
1. **No two consecutive days** can have the same color.
2. There should not exist four days \( a, b, c, d \) such that \( a < b < c < d \) and the bird has the same color on days \( a \) and \( c \), and the same color on days \( b \) and \( d \), different from the colors on days \( a \) and \( c \).

### Key Insight:
The problem is equivalent to constructing a Davenport-Schinzel sequence of order 3, which avoids any alternating subsequence of two different colors of length 4 (i.e., \( X, Y, X, Y \)).

### Solution Approach:
1. **Understanding Davenport-Schinzel Sequences**:
   - A Davenport-Schinzel sequence of order 3 avoids any subsequence of the form \( X, Y, X, Y \).
   - For \( n \) symbols, the maximum length of such a sequence is given by \( \lambda_3(n) \), which is approximately \( 2n - 1 \).

2. **Constructing the Sequence**:
   - For \( n = 2 \):
     - Maximum days: \( 2 \times 2 - 1 = 3 \).
     - Valid sequence: \( A, B, A \).
   - For \( n = 3 \):
     - Maximum days: \( 2 \times 3 - 1 = 5 \).
     - Valid sequence: \( A, B, C, B, A \).
   - For \( n = 4 \):
     - Maximum days: \( 2 \times 4 - 1 = 7 \).
     - Valid sequence: \( A, B, C, D, C, B, A \).

3. **General Formula**:
   - The general formula for the maximum number of days is \( 2n - 1 \).

### Verification:
- For \( n = 2 \):
  - Valid sequence: \( A, B, A \).
  - No forbidden quartet: \( (A, B, A) \) does not form \( X, Y, X, Y \).
- For \( n = 3 \):
  - Valid sequence: \( A, B, C, B, A \).
  - No forbidden quartet: Checking quartets like \( (A, B, B, A) \) and \( (B, C, B, A) \) shows no forbidden pattern.
- For \( n = 4 \):
  - Valid sequence: \( A, B, C, D, C, B, A \).
  - No forbidden quartet: Checking quartets like \( (A, B, C, B) \) and \( (B, C, D, B) \) shows no forbidden pattern.

### Conclusion:
The maximum number of days Rainbow can live, given \( n \) colors, while adhering to the constraints, is \( 2n - 1 \).

#include <bits/stdc++.h>
using namespace std;

vector<int> computeFailure(const string& pat) {
    int m = pat.size();
    vector<int> fail(m, 0);
    for (int i = 1; i < m; ++i) {
        int j = fail[i-1];
        while (j > 0 && pat[i] != pat[j]) {
            j = fail[j-1];
        }
        if (pat[i] == pat[j]) {
            j++;
        }
        fail[i] = j;
    }
    return fail;
}

int main() {
    ios_base::sync_with_stdio(false);
    cin.tie(nullptr);
    
    string s;
    cin >> s;
    int q;
    cin >> q;
    
    while (q--) {
        int type;
        cin >> type;
        if (type == 1) {
            int i; char c;
            cin >> i >> c;
            s[i-1] = c;
        } else {
            int l, r;
            string y;
            cin >> l >> r >> y;
            int m = y.size();
            int len = r - l + 1;
            if (m > len) {
                cout << 0 << '\n';
                continue;
            }
            int ans = 0;
            if (m <= 20) {
                int start = l - 1;
                int end = r - m;
                if (start <= end) {
                    for (int i = start; i <= end; ++i) {
                        bool match = true;
                        for (int j = 0; j < m; ++j) {
                            if (i + j >= s.size() || s[i + j] != y[j]) {
                                match = false;
                                break;
                            }
                        }
                        if (match) {
                            ans++;
                        }
                    }
                }
            } else {
                vector<int> fail = computeFailure(y);
                int current = 0;
                int start_l = l - 1;
                int end_r = r - 1;
                int max_start = end_r - m + 1;
                for (int pos = start_l; pos <= end_r; ++pos) {
                    char c = s[pos];
                    while (current > 0 && y[current] != c) {
                        current = fail[current - 1];
                    }
                    if (y[current] == c) {
                        current++;
                    }
                    if (current == m) {
                        int start_pos = pos - m + 1;
                        if (start_pos >= start_l && start_pos <= max_start) {
                            ans++;
                        }
                        current = fail[current - 1];
                    }
                }
            }
            cout << ans << '\n';
        }
    }
    
    return 0;

The intersection point in the direction towards (-2,11) should be the closest point Q.

Drawing a line between these two points, the closest point Q on the circle should lie somewhere along this line segment between the center and (-2,11), but only 5 units away from the center since the radius is 5. So the distance between the center and (-2,11) is 10 units. The radius is 5, so the external point is 10 units away, which is twice the radius. So Q is exactly halfway between (6,5) and (-2,11). Wait, is that correct?

Since the distance between the center and the external point is 10, which is more than the radius 5, so the closest point is the point on the line connecting them, 5 units away from the center towards the external point.

So the vector from the center to the external point is (-2 -6, 11 -5) = (-8,6). The direction vector is (-8,6). The unit vector in that direction would be (-8,6) divided by its magnitude. The magnitude is 10, as we found earlier. So the unit vector is (-8/10, 6/10) = (-4/5, 3/5). So moving 5 units from the center in this direction would give the point Q.

Then plugging back into the parametric equations: x=6 -8*(1/2)=6-4=2, y=5 +6*(1/2)=5+3=8. So that gives Q=(2,8) again. So that seems consistent.

1. **Identify the center and radius of the circle:**
   The circle is centered at \((6, 5)\) with a radius of 5.

2. **Determine the direction vector from the center to the external point:**
   The direction vector from the center \((6, 5)\) to the external point \((-2, 11)\) is:
   \[
   (-2 - 6, 11 - 5) = (-8, 6)
   \]

   The magnitude of the direction vector \((-8, 6)\) is:
   \[
   \sqrt{(-8)^2 + 6^2} = \sqrt{64 + 36} = \sqrt{100} = 10
   \]
   The unit vector in this direction is:
   \[
   \left( \frac{-8}{10}, \frac{6}{10} \right) = \left( -\frac{4}{5}, \frac{3}{5} \right)
   \]

   The coordinates of the point \( Q \) on the circle in the direction of the external point can be found by scaling the unit vector by the radius (5) and adding it to the center \((6, 5)\):
   \[

The code can do the following:

Given a list of first (d+1) terms s[:d+1].

Then construct the matrix of size (d+1)x(d+1), where each row i is:

row[i] = [n^0, n^1, ..., n^d] for n=i.

Wait, n starts at 0, so rows are:

for row number 0 (n=0):

   1 0 0 0 ... 

for row 1 (n=1):

   1, 1, 1^2, 1^3,...

row 2 (n=2):

   1, 2, 4, etc.

Up to row d (n=d).

The vector to solve is the s[0], s[1], ..., s[d].

Once the coefficients a's are determined via solving the system, that's the desired coefficient list.

So how to solve the linear system?

Possible way with numpy's linalg.solve function, but in code golf, perhaps the language is chosen such as Python, but even there, code length may be an issue.

However, in code golf, sometimes the standard approach is to find a way to express the calculation without heavy linear algebra.

Alternatively, in case the rows are from n=0 to n=d:

We can use the fact that the first row is [1 0 ...] so the first equation sets a0 = s[0], then substitute a0 into the next equations.

The second equation is a0 + a1 *1 +a2*1... = s[1]

So a1 = s[1] - a0 - a2*1^2 ...? Not helpful.

Alternatively, using Gaussian elimination manually for code-golf purposes.

But coding Gaussian elimination is a bit involved.

For a code-golfed approach, perhaps for m terms, building the system and using a library function is feasible, like in Python using numpy, but I don't know if that's acceptable here. 

Alternatively, note that in k-th row of the matrix row k is the vector for x^0 + x^1 *k ... up to k^d.

Solving for the coefficients can perhaps be dome using determinants, but Cramers rule is computationally heavier.

a0 = s[0]

a2 = ( s[2] - a0 - a1*2 ) / (2^2 - 2*1 ? Not sure.

Let me think of code approaches.

The first part is to compute the degree d:

Implementing the difference tables until all elements are the same:

Here's how to implemented:

Start with the list of terms.

For each step, compute the difference list between consecutive terms.

Check if all entries in it are equal.

We can proceed until we find the step where this is true.

Once found, the degree is the number of steps taken.

Steps take until you've found all Same.

For example:

def find_degree(terms):

   current = terms

   degree =0

   while True:

       next_diff = [current[i] - current[i-1] for i in range(1, len(current))]

       if all(x == next_diff[0] for x in next_diff):

           return degree

       current = next_diff

       degree +=1 

Wait but wait that logic is for ints:

Suppose next_diff is a list of length L = len(current) -1.

We are checking whether all elements are the same.

 If yes → degree is the previous steps?

Wait in the function above:

Initially, we have current as the input, degree starts at 0.

Then in first iteration:

 compute next_diff → this is first differences.

 Then check if next_diff is all same → if yes, then return degree (which is 0). But that is not correct.

 Wait initial degree starts as ZERO. But the first next_diff is the first difference, so if that first_diff has all same elements, then the polynomial which gives that is linear?

 Wait in the example [2,2,2]:

The first differences are [0,0], so next_diff is [0,0]. The function proceeds to check:

next_diff elements are all same (0 ==0), so returns degree 0. Which is correct, because the polynomial is degree 0.

Ah, the degree is the steps taken before the first valid. 

Thus the function is correct.

Another test case for degree1: [1,2,3]

Initial current is [1,2,3], degree 0.

 first_diff is [1,1]. all same → returns 0? No.

But should be degree1? Wait no! 

Wait in this case, the first difference (first_diff) has elements all the same : yes → returns degree 0. So function in that case would say the degree is zero? That's wrong.

Wait wait what's the correct degree for [1,2,3]? It is a linear polynomial, degree1.

The first differences (next_diff) are [1,1]. all same → so the previously described function returns 0. Therefore the function is incorrect.

Ahoh! Here's the problem. So the function's logic is faulty.

The function assumes that when the differences are all same elements at step 'current', then the polynomial is degree 0. But in the above example, 'current' is the first differences, but degree is step?

No, the problem is that we have to count the steps taken to get the level where the differences are constant.

In the [1,2,3] example, the first difference level 1 (after one step) is [1,1] which is all same elements. The degree should be 1. But the function returns degree =0 because 'degree' was incremented after returning?

Wait the progress:

current was the initial terms → the 0th differences.

then:

compute next_diff → first differences (level 1)

then check if that next_diff is all same (yes).

Thus, function returns degree (which was initialized to 0) → but it should return 1 as the degree.

Ah, the function returns degree which was initial 0, but the step count needs increments after the first loop.

The function is wrong. Let's see:

Inside the function code block:

   degree =0

   while True:

       compute next_diff

       if the condition:

          return degree

       else:

          current = next_diff

          degree +=1 

Wait, the first loop:

In the first iteration of the while loop:

current holds the original terms.

then compute next_diff (differences between terms).

Check if all elements are same: if yes → returns degree (0).

But that's incorrect, because the original terms weren't all the same.

Ah! So the condition must be first for the current level.

Wait no. 

The first difference may be the first possible level (level 1), .

 The degree is the level where the differences became same.

 Thus:

The function above is fully flawed.

The problem is that the first check is whether the next_diff (level1) has same elements.

 We认为 degree is then 0, but the degree should be the level of next_diff → degree is 1-0?

等待.

The correct approach should be that the degree is the current level of the difference (number of steps taken to get here).

Thus, perhaps the steps are:

initially:

 degree is zero, but the initial check skips?

 Wait perhaps the level is tracked as the number of steps taken to compute next_diff.

 Thus:

Upon entry to the loop:

 Compute next_diff (level 1).

 if next_diff is same → then degree is 1?

 或者 no.

 Let's try to recast the function.

#include <bits/stdc++.h>
using namespace std;

int solve(const vector<int>& a, int x, int y) {
    int n = a.size();
    int count_x = 0, count_y = 0;
    unordered_map<int, int> other;
    int max_other = 0;
    int max_len = 0;
    int left = 0;

    for (int right = 0; right < n; ++right) {
        int c = a[right];
        if (c == x) {
            count_x++;
        } else if (c == y) {
            count_y++;
        } else {
            other[c]++;
            if (other[c] > max_other) {
                max_other = other[c];
            }
        }

        while (left <= right) {
            bool valid = (count_x == count_y) && (max_other <= count_x);
            if (valid) break;

            int d = a[left];
            if (d == x) {
                count_x--;
            } else if (d == y) {
                count_y--;
            } else {
                other[d]--;
                if (other[d] == 0) {
                    other.erase(d);
                    if (max_other == other[d] + 1) {
                        max_other = 0;
                        for (const auto& p : other) {
                            if (p.second > max_other) {
                                max_other = p.second;
                            }
                        }
                    }
                } else {
                    if (other[d] + 1 == max_other) {
                        max_other--;
                    }
                }
            }
            left++;
        }

        if (count_x == count_y && count_x > 0) {
            max_len = max(max_len, right - left + 1);
        }
    }

    return max_len;
}

int main() {
    ios_base::sync_with_stdio(false);
    cin.tie(NULL);

    int n;
    cin >> n;
    vector<int> a(n);
    for (int i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
        cin >> a[i];
    }

    if (n <= 1) {
        cout << 0 << '\n';
        return 0;
    }

    unordered_map<int, int> freq;
    for (int x : a) {
        freq[x]++;
    }

    vector<pair<int, int>> elements;
    for (auto& p : freq) {
        elements.emplace_back(-p.second, p.first);
    }
    sort(elements.begin(), elements.end());

    vector<int> candidates;
    for (auto& p : elements) {
        candidates.push_back(p.second);
        if (candidates.size() >= 200) {
            break;
        }
    }

    int max_len = 0;
    for (int x : candidates) {
        for (int y : candidates) {
            if (x == y) continue;
            int current = solve(a, x, y);
            if (current > max_len) {
                max_len = current;
            }
        }
    }

    bool all_distinct = (freq.size() == n);
    if (all_distinct) {
        max_len = n;
    } else {
        for (int x : candidates) {
            int current = solve(a, x, x);
            if (current > max_len) {
                max_len = current;
            }
        }
        for (auto& p : freq) {
            if (p.second >= 2) {
                max_len = max(max_len, 2);
                break;
            }
        }
    }

    cout << max_len << '\n';

    return 0;
}<#sep#> \) on the circle in the direction of the external point can be found by scaling the unit vector by the radius (5) and adding it to the center \((6, 5)\):
   \[
   Q = (6, 5) + 5 \left( -\frac{4}{5}, \frac{3}{5} \right) = (6, 5) + (-4, 3) = (2, 8)
   \]

5. **Verify that the point \( Q \) lies on the circle:**
   Substitute \( (2, 8) \) into the circle's equation:
   \[
   (2 - 6)^2 + (8 - 5)^2 = (-4)^2 + 3^2 = 16 + 9 = 25
   \]
   The point \((2, 8)\) satisfies the equation of the circle.

6. **Verify the distance from \( Q \) to the external point:**
   The distance from \( Q = (2, 8) \) to the external point \((-2, 11)\) is:
   \[
   \sqrt{(2 - (-2))^2 + (8 - 11)^2} = \sqrt{4^2 + (-3)^2} = \sqrt{16 + 9} = \sqrt{25} = 5
   \]
   This distance is equal to the radius of the circle, confirming that \( Q \) is indeed the closest point on the circle to \((-2, 11)\).

Therefore, the coordinates of the point \( Q \) are:

        if all(x == diff[0] for x in diff):

            return level +1

Wait, because the first step (compute level1 -> first differences), thus if returns at this step, then level was initially 0 → returns 1. That fixes the prior example.

Let me test with the [1,2,3] example:

 Initial current is [1,2,3], level is 0.

 Compute diff = [1,1].

 all same → return level +1 → which is 0+1 =1 → correct.

 In the [2,2,2] example:

 first computes diff [0,0], which is all same → returns level (0) +1 =1? Which is wrong, as the degree is 0.

 this is a problem.

 Ah right.

So the condtion has to distinguish between cases where the initial terms are constant.

 Suppose:

 [2,2,2]: current is [2,2,2] → level starts at zero.

Its difference is computed: diff is [0,0]. 

The switch is 'if all elements of diff are same' → yes → returns 0+1 →1.

But in reality the'correct degree is zero.

The problem is that the initial current is the 0th differences, which has length 3. We cannot conclude the degree is zero unless all the elements of the 0th differences are same (the initial terms) → thus, we must check that first.

 Thus, the logic must first check the 0th differences (current) whether they are all same?

 The previous function didn't do that.

Therefore, the correct algorithm is:

计算 each possible level, starting with level 0 (当前的序列), level 1, etc.

At each step:

如果当前的矢量 itself is all the same:

    然后 the degree is 0 .

但是, need to check this before moving to higher levels.

 所以 the algorithm진ly:

The degree is the smallest d for which the d-th level differences are consistent.

atch level 0:

if the original terms are all the same:

→ degree 0.

 otherwise:

 hover authentication to level 1, and so on.

Thus, 修正后的函数:

def find_degree(terms):

    current = terms

    level =0 

    while True:

        # 查看 current是否有 all same elements .

        if all( x == current[0] for x in current):

            return level

        # compute next difference level

        diff = [ current[i] - current[i-1] for i in 1 range(, len(current)) ]

        current = diff 

        level +=1 

这样：

对于例 如[2,2,2] → current is all same → return level0 → correct.

对于 [1,2,3]:

 current (0层) is [1,2,3] → not all same. Then compute level 1:

diff 是 [1,1]. 

此时，进入下 ninth iteration:, current is diff → [1,1].

 随后 d检查 whether they are all same → yes → return → level1 →正确.

, so这个 function works.

So the degree is the first level这一，当 all the terms of current are same통.

这样 the function is correct.

Now, the next step is to compute the coefficients.

 Once we have the degree d, 

我们需要 the first d+1 terms of the initial sequence.

因为 the minimal polynomial is:

the polynomial of degree d that fits the first d+1 terms (因为 with m terms, there are enough points).

 We can take the first d+1 terms — since the polynomial is the minimal degree to fit the sequence, it will automatically fit all the terms.

 So once we have the initial d+1 terms:

We can set up the system of equations to solve for the coefficients a_0,...a_d .

The equations are:

 For each n in 0到d:

   a0 + a1*n ... + a_d *n^d = s[n]

The matrix is a Vandermond matrix.

 solving this system gives the coefficients.

 To do this computation in code:

We can form the matrix and then solve it using whatever method is feasible.

In code golf, perhaps using matrix inversion:

But in Python, without using numpy, it's possible to do with built-in functions?

Alternatively, use Gaussian elimination.

Alternatively, find a recursive method for small degrees:

 : which might be difficult but possibly golfable.

Let's consider implementing Gaussian elimination ~.

The system is equations Ax = B.

Where:

A is a matrix of size (d+1)马d+1行), where each row is multiples:

Row i, column j = (i)^j

Wait the rows are numbered 0到d:

 for row number i (starting at 0), each column 0到d is the exponential term up to column j:

 for column 0: 1 (i^0).

 Column 1: i^1, etc.

 Thus, the row is [i^0, i^1, ..., i^d]

The vector B is the first d+1 terms of the input.

We need to solve Ax = B for x.

 Implementing Gaussian elimination with partial pivoting.

 This could be doable, but requires coding the steps.

 For example, in pseudocode:

 After building matrix A and vector B:

 perform Gaussian elimination to upper triangular form, in place.

 then back substitution.

 \\ perhaps this is the way to go.

Once the matrix is in upper triangular form, then:

 for i from d down to 0:

     x[i] = (B[i] - sum_{j=i+1 to d}(A[i][j] * x_j )) / A[i][i].

Thus, the code steps could be:

 1. Build the system matrix and B.

 2. Perform Gaussian elimination to row-echelon form.

 3. Backwards substitute to find coefficients.

 The code for this is going to be a bit verbose, but perhaps it can be golfed.

 For example, in Python, the following steps (assuming that d is the degree):

First, taking the first d+1 terms from the input:

terms = input_sequence[:d+1]

Then, build the matrix and B vector.

矩阵列 is列 for each term's exponents:

 n的 values are the indices themselves (0 ⇌, d.

 Thus, matrix has rows of [n^k for k in 0到d].

 For example, d=1, the row for n=0 is [1,0], for n=1 [1,1].

 Po对此 code:

  M = []

  for n in range(d+1):

      row = [n**k for k in range(d+1)]

      M.append(row)

   B is the first d+1 terms.

Then, we need to solve this system.

 implementing Gaussian elimination:

 in code:

 for i in range(len(M)):

     # 获取 leading column entering the pivot

     pivot = M[i][i]

     if abs(pivot) < 1e-9: 这 should not happen, since the matrix is non-singular for degree d.

     for j in range(i+1, len(M)):

         r = M[j][i]/pivot

         for k in range(i, len(M)+1):

             M[j][k] -= r * M[i][k]

             # B vector is embedded in the matrix's augmented column. So we need to combine the matrix and B.

 perhaps the B is extended as part of the matrix.

Thus, better to have the augmented matrix:

A的 augmented matrix is the M with columns plus the B terms.

 augmented = [[M[i][j] for j in range(d+1)]+ [B[i]] for i in range(d+1)]

然后 perform ensures:

 for row in 0到d:

      pivot = augmented[i][i]

      对于 each row below:

         factor = A[row][i]/pivot

         subtract factor * row i from row j.

 This will take time, but for small d (up到 say 8 or 9), it's manageable.

 Once augmented in row-echelon form (upper triangular), then substitute:

 x = [0]*(d+1)

 for i in reversed(range(d+1)):

    s =0 

    for j in range(i+1, d+1):

        s += augmented[i][j]*x[j]

    x[i] = (augmented[i][d+1] - s) / augmented[i][i]

 Then return x.

 So putting it all together in Python.

但是 this is going to be quite long for code golf.

Perhaps we can find a better approach.

 Another idea: the coefficients can be directly computed from the difference table:

 The first elements of the difference levels give the coefficients of the newton binomial expansion, which can be converted to the standard form.

 For the case of getting the coefficients -> 通过 Newton的 interpolation formula:

p(n) = ∑_{k=0}^d<#sep#> used to parse the token stream and call our listener.

### SIMPLIFYING ALGEBRAIC FRACTIONS

Tallahassee Community College 5 SIMPLIFYING ALGEBRAIC F<#sep#>-

#include <bits/stdc++.h>
using namespace std;

struct State {
    long long a, b, c, sum2;
    string path;
};

struct SecondPartEntry {
    long long sum2;
    long long a, b, c;
    string path;
    SecondPartEntry() : sum2(-1e18), a(0), b(0), c(0), path("") {}
};

vector<State> generate_states(const vector<tuple<int, int, int>>& quests) {
    vector<State> states;
    states.push_back({0, 0, 0, 0, ""});
    for (const auto& q : quests) {
        int l = get<0>(q);
        int m = get<1>(q);
        int w = get<2>(q);
        vector<State> new_states;
        for (const auto& s : states) {
            // LM
            new_states.push_back({s.a + l, s.b + m, s.c, s.sum2 + l + m, s.path + "LM"});
            // LW
            new_states.push_back({s.a + l, s.b, s.c + w, s.sum2 + l + w, s.path + "LW"});
            // MW
            new_states.push_back({s.a, s.b + m, s.c + w, s.sum2 + m + w, s.path + "MW"});
        }
        states.swap(new_states);
    }
    return states;
}

int main() {
    int n;
    cin >> n;
    vector<tuple<int, int, int>> quests(n);
    for (int i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
        int l, m, w;
        cin >> l >> m >> w;
        quests[i] = {l, m, w};
    }
    int k = n / 2;
    vector<tuple<int, int, int>> first_quests(quests.begin(), quests.begin() + k);
    vector<tuple<int, int, int>> second_quests(quests.begin() + k, quests.end());

    auto first_states = generate_states(first_quests);
    auto second_states = generate_states(second_quests);

    using Key = pair<long long, long long>;
    using EntryArray = array<SecondPartEntry, 3>;
    map<Key, EntryArray> second_map;

    for (const auto& s : second_states) {
        long long a2 = s.a, b2 = s.b, c2 = s.c;
        long long diff_ab = b2 - a2;
        long long diff_ac = c2 - a2;
        Key key = {diff_ab, diff_ac};
        long long sum2_2 = s.sum2;
        int rem = sum2_2 % 3;
        if (rem < 0) rem += 3;
        auto& entry = second_map[key][rem];
        if (sum2_2 > entry.sum2) {
            entry.sum2 = sum2_2;
            entry.a = a2;
            entry.b = b2;
            entry.c = c2;
            entry.path = s.path;
        }
    }

    long long max_s = -1e18;
    string best_path;

    for (const auto& s1 : first_states) {
        long long a1 = s1.a, b1 = s1.b, c1 = s1.c, sum2_1 = s1.sum2;
        long long diff_ab = a1 - b1;
        long long diff_ac = a1 - c1;
        Key key = {diff_ab, diff_ac};
        auto it = second_map.find(key);
        if (it == second_map.end()) continue;

        int required_rem = (-sum2_1) % 3;
        if (required_rem < 0) required_rem += 3;
        const auto& entry = it->second[required_rem];
        if (entry.sum2 == -1e18) continue;

        long long sum_total = sum2_1 + entry.sum2;
        if (sum_total % 3 != 0) continue;
        long long s = sum_total / 3;

        if (a1 + entry.a != s || b1 + entry.b != s || c1 + entry.c != s)
            continue;

        if (s > max_s) {
            max_s = s;
            best_path = s1.path + entry.path;
        }
    }

    if (max_s == -1e18) {
        cout << "Impossible" << endl;
    } else {
        for (size_t i = 0; i < best_path.size(); i += 2) {
            cout << best_path.substr(i, 2) << endl;
        }
    }

    return 0;
}<#sep#>If $f(x) = -\dfrac{1}{x},$ what is $f(f(f(f(f(6)))))$?

<think>
Okay, so I need to find f(f(f(f(f(6))))) where f(x) is -1/x. Let's take this step by step. Maybe if I compute each application of f one by one, it'll make sense.

First, let's start with the innermost function: f(6). Since f(x) = -1/x, substituting 6 in, that would be -1/6. So f(6) is -1/6. Got that.

Next, I need to apply f again to this result. So f(f(6)) is f(-1/6). Plugging -1/6 into f(x), which is -1 divided by x. So, that's -1 divided by (-1/6). Dividing by a fraction is the same as multiplying by its reciprocal, so -1 ÷ (-1/6) = -1 * (-6/1) = 6. Wait, that's positive 6? Let me check: negative divided by negative is positive, and 1/(1/6) is 6. So yes, that's 6. So f(f(6)) is 6.

Hmm, interesting. Now, applying f again to that result. So f(f(f(6))) would be f(6). Wait, but we already calculated f(6) earlier as -1/6. So this is the same as the first step. So this seems like a cycle here. Let me confirm:

f(6) = -1/6

f(f(6)) = f(-1/6) = 6

f(f(f(6))) = f(6) = -1/6

f(f(f(f(6)))) = f(-1/6) = 6

f(f(f(f(f(6))))) = f(6) = -1/6

Wait a minute, so it's alternating between -1/6 and 6 each time we apply the function f. Let's count the number of applications. The original problem is f(f(f(f(f(6))))) which is five applications. Starting with 6, applying f five times.

Let's list them step by step:

1st f: f(6) = -1/6

2nd f: f(-1/6) = 6

3rd f: f(6) = -1/6

4th f: f(-1/6) = 6

5th f: f(6) = -1/6

So after 5 applications, it's back to -1/6. Therefore, the answer should be -1/6.

But wait, let me make sure I didn't make a mistake in counting. Let's write each step:

1. f(6) = -1/6 (1st)

2. f(f(6)) = f(-1/6) = 6 (2nd)

3. f(f(f(6))) = f(6) = -1/6 (3rd)

4. f^4(6) = f(-1/6) = 6 (4th)

5. f^5(6) = f(6) = -1/6 (5th)

Yes, so fifth application is -1/6. Therefore, the answer is -1/6. Let me just check once again to prevent any off-by-one errors. Since each pair of applications brings us back to the original number, odd number of applications would end up at -1/6, and even at 6. Since 5 is odd, yes, the result is -1/6.

Alternatively, maybe there's a pattern here. Let me see:

If you apply f once: -1/6

Twice: 6

Three times: -1/6

Four times: 6

Five times: -1/6

So it's a cycle of 2. Therefore, the nth application depends on whether n is odd or even. Since 5 is odd, we end up at -1/6. Therefore, the final answer is -1/6.

I think that's solid. Let me just do the computations once again without skipping steps to confirm.

First application:

f(6) = -1/6.

Second:

f(-1/6) = -1 / (-1/6) = (-1) * (-6/1) = 6.

Third:

f(6) = -1/6 (same as first step)

Fourth:

f(-1/6) = 6 (same as second step)

Fifth:

f(6) = -1/6.

Yep, exactly. So after five times, it's definitely -1/6. So the answer is -1/6. That seems right.
</think>To solve the problem of finding \( f(f(f(f(f(6))))) \) where \( f(x) = -\frac{1}{x} \), we need to apply the function \( f \) step by step.

1. Compute the first application of \( f \):
   \[
   f(6) = -\frac{1}{6}
   \]

2. Compute the second application of \( f \):
   \[
   f\left( -\frac{1}{6} \right) = -\frac{1}{-\frac{1}{6}} = 6
   \]

3. Compute the third application of \( f \):
   \[
   f(6) = -\frac{1}{6}
   \]

4. Compute the fourth application of \( f \):
   \[
   f\left( -\frac{1}{6} \right) = 6
   \]

5. Compute the fifth application of \( f \):
   \[
   f(6) = -\frac{1}{6}
   \]

From the above steps, we observe that the function \( f \) alternates between \( -\frac{1}{6} \) and \( 6 \). Specifically:
- After an odd number of applications, the result is \( -\frac{1}{6} \).
- After an even number of applications, the result is \( 6 \).

Since we are applying the function 5 times (an odd number), the final result is:
\[
f(f(f(f(f(6))))) = -\frac{1}{6}
\]

Thus, the answer is:
\[
\boxed{-\frac{1}{6}}
\]<#sep#>j]*x[j]

    x[i] = (augmented[i][d+1] - s) / augmented[i][i]

 Then return x.

 So putting it all together in Python.

但是 this is going to be quite long for code golf.

Perhaps we can find a better approach.

 Another idea: the coefficients can be directly computed from the difference table:

 The first elements of the difference levels give the coefficients of the newton binomial expansion, which can be converted to the standard form.

 For the case of getting the coefficients -> 通过 Newton的 interpolation formula:

p(n) = ∑_{k=0}^d c_k * C(n, k)

问题 now is to expand this sum into the standard polynomial form.

To compute this expansion efficiently without fractions, we can compute each term's contribution as an integer polynomial, but scaled by k! to avoid fractions. Then divide appropriately. 

 But this may not be simple.

 However, the coefficients can be computed via:

 The coefficient of n^i is sum_{k=i}^d (c_k * S_二项式 coefficient of n^i in C(n,k))).

 The binomial coefficient C(n,k) can be represented as a polynomial with states總:

 C(n, k) = (n (n-1) ... (n-k+1)) /k! 

 Thus, 機 n terms, the coefficient of n^i is および binomial coefficients의组合.

 This is getting too complicated for code golf.

 Perhaps the minimal way is to proceed with the system of equations method.

 Thus, in 'Python', here's a possible code outline:

First, compute the degree d:

 We need a function to compute degree as in the earlier corrected algorithm.

 Then, solve the system.

Below is the code outline:

#include <bits/stdc++.h>
using namespace std;

struct Edge {
    int to, rev, cap, cost;
    Edge(int t, int r, int c, int co) : to(t), rev(r), cap(c), cost(co) {}
};

class MinCostFlow {
public:
    int n;
    vector<vector<Edge>> g;
    vector<int> h, dist, prevv, preve;
    
    MinCostFlow(int size) : n(size), g(size) {}
    
    void add_edge(int from, int to, int cap, int cost) {
        g[from].emplace_back(to, g[to].size(), cap, cost);
        g[to].emplace_back(from, g[from].size()-1, 0, -cost);
    }
    
    int compute(int s, int t, int f) {
        int res = 0;
        h.assign(n, 0);
        prevv.assign(n, -1);
        preve.assign(n, -1);
        
        while (f > 0) {
            priority_queue<pair<int, int>, vector<pair<int, int>>, greater<pair<int, int>>> pq;
            dist.assign(n, numeric_limits<int>::max());
            dist[s] = 0;
            pq.push({0, s});
            
            while (!pq.empty()) {
                auto [d, v] = pq.top();
                pq.pop();
                if (dist[v] < d) continue;
                for (size_t i = 0; i < g[v].size(); i++) {
                    Edge& e = g[v][i];
                    if (e.cap > 0 && dist[e.to] > d + e.cost + h[v] - h[e.to]) {
                        dist[e.to] = d + e.cost + h[v] - h[e.to];
                        prevv[e.to] = v;
                        preve[e.to] = i;
                        pq.push({dist[e.to], e.to});
                    }
                }
            }
            
            if (dist[t] == numeric_limits<int>::max()) return -1;
            
            for (int v = 0; v < n; v++)
                if (h[v] < numeric_limits<int>::max()) h[v] += dist[v];
            
            int d = f;
            for (int v = t; v != s; v = prevv[v])
                d = min(d, g[prevv[v]][preve[v]].cap);
            f -= d;
            res += d * h[t];
            for (int v = t; v != s; v = prevv[v]) {
                Edge& e = g[prevv[v]][preve[v]];
                e.cap -= d;
                g[v][e.rev].cap += d;
            }
        }
        return res;
    }
};

int main() {
    int n, m;
    cin >> n >> m;
    vector<string> s(n);
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) cin >> s[i];
    vector<vector<int>> a(n, vector<int>(m));
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++)
        for (int j = 0; j < m; j++)
            cin >> a[i][j];
    
    int source = 0, sink = 1 + n + m * 26;
    MinCostFlow mcf(sink + 1);
    
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++)
        mcf.add_edge(source, i + 1, 1, 0);
    
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++)
        for (int j = 0; j < m; j++) {
            char oc = s[i][j];
            for (char c = 'a'; c <= 'z'; c++) {
                int cost = (c == oc) ? 0 : a[i][j];
                int jc_node = 1 + n + j * 26 + (c - 'a');
                mcf.add_edge(i + 1, jc_node, 1, cost);
            }
        }
    
    for (int j = 0; j < m; j++)
        for (char c = 'a'; c <= 'z'; c++) {
            int jc_node = 1 + n + j * 26 + (c - 'a');
            mcf.add_edge(jc_node, sink, 1, 0);
        }
    
    cout << mcf.compute(source, sink, n) << endl;

Then 3f(<#sep#>human: Lunar New Year is approaching, and Bob received a gift from his friend recently — a recursive sequence! He loves this sequence very much and wants to play with it.

Let f_1, f_2, …, f_i, … be an infinite sequence of positive integers. Bob knows that for i > k, f_i can be obtained by the following recursive equation:

$$$f_i = \left(f_{i - 1} ^ {b_1} ⋅ f_{i - 2} ^ {b_2} ⋅ ⋅⋅⋅ ⋅ f_{i - k} ^ {b_k}\right) mod p,$$$

which in short is

$$$f_i = \left(∏_{j = 1}^{k} f_{i - j}^{b_j}\right) mod p,$$$

where p = 998 244 353 (a widely-used prime), b_1, b_2, …, b_k are known integer constants, and x mod y denotes the remainder of x divided by y.

Bob lost the values of f_1, f_2, …, f_k, which is extremely troublesome – these are the basis of the sequence! Luckily, Bob remembers the first k - 1 elements of the sequence: f_1 = f_2 = … = f_{k - 1} = 1 and the n-th element: f_n = m. Please find any possible value of f_k. If no solution exists, just tell Bob that it is impossible to recover his favorite sequence, regardless of Bob's sadness.
Input

The first line contains a positive integer k (1 ≤ k ≤ 100), denoting the length of the sequence b_1, b_2, …, b_k.

The second line contains k positive integers b_1, b_2, …, b_k (1 ≤ b_i < p).

The third line contains two positive integers n and m (k < n ≤ 10^9, 1 ≤ m < p), which implies f_n = m.

Output

Output a possible value of f_k, where f_k is a positive integer satisfying 1 ≤ f_k < p. If there are multiple answers, print any of them. If no such f_k makes f_n = m, output -1 instead.

It is easy to show that if there are some possible values of f_k, there must be at least one satisfying 1 ≤ f_k < p.

Examples

Input


3
2 3 5
4 16


Output


4


Input


5
4 7 1 5 6
7 14187219


Output


6


Input


8
2 3 5 6 1 7 9 10
23333 1


Output


1


Input


1
2
88888 66666


Output


-1


Input


3
998244352 998244352 998244352
4 2


Output


-1


Input


10
283 463 213 777 346 201 463 283 102 999
2333333 6263423


Output


382480067

Note

In the first sample, we have f_4 = f_3^2 ⋅ f_2^3 ⋅ f_1^5. Therefore, applying f_3 = 4, we have f_4 = 16. Note that there can be multiple answers.

In the third sample, applying f_7 = 1 makes f_{23333} = 1.

In the fourth sample, no such f_1 makes f_{88888} = 66666. Therefore, we output -1 instead.

gpt: <think> Okay, let's see. I need to solve this problem where Bob lost the initial terms of his recursive sequence, and I have to find possible f_k. Hmm, the problem seems a bit complicated at first, but maybe I can break it down step by step.

So, the sequence is defined recursively for i>k: each term is the product of the previous k terms each raised to some exponent b_j, mod p. The first k-1 terms are known to be 1, except f_k which is the unknown we need to find. And we also know that the nth term is m. The task is to find any valid f_k or say it's impossible.

<#sep#>Music theory for nerds (eev.ee) 766 points by hardmath123 on Sept 19, 2016 | hide | past | web | favorite | 378 comments

 I'd like to elaborate a bit, in the same "for nerds" manner, on where Eevee seems to get lost a bit with scales and notation. He (she? not sure) calls the A minor and C major scales the same, because they contain the same notes. That's not an odd thought, but it's like calling sine and cosine the same because both functions contain the same set of values, in the same order.The difference is phase. Basically, scales aren't just an ordered set of notes, they also have a starting point. This note, the note the scale is named after, is often the last note of a tune played in that scale, and often the first too (especially for more poppy tunes). So if you play Für Elise in C major, like Eevee suggests, the entire melody will be pitched 3 semitones higher than playing Für Elise in A major. And it'll sound awkward because you're supposed to play it on a minor scale.Once you understand this, the whole notation thing makes a lot more sense as well.
 Even though the key signature for a major and its relative minor are the same, that is only part of the story.When you see that A Minor has the same key signature as C Major, what you are seeing is the "natural minor" scale. This is simply the notes implied by the key signature, and so is identical (but down a third) from the relative major. If you play purely in the natural minor, you are really closer to the Aeolian mode than a minor key.There is also the "harmonic minor" and "melodic minor" which, in common practice period, are much more commonly used than natural minor. Harmonic minor has a raised seventh. This makes the dominant (V) triad a major triad which increases its need to resolve to the tonic (I). It is used for harmonies (obviously based on the name) for this reason, but it makes the step from 6 to 7 an augmented second (i.e. minor third) so its isn't used melodically very often. Which is why there is also a Melodic Minor.Melodic minor is tricky because it varies depending on whether the melody is ascending or descending. Descending is easy, it is identical to natural minor. Ascending is similar to the harmonic minor with its raised seventh (creating what is known as a leading tone, i.e. a half step below the tonic that wants to resolve up to the tonic.) But the ascending scale also has a raised sixth, which eliminates the augmented second between 6 and 7.
 I've never quite understood why you learn a melodic minor as being different depending on if it's ascending or descending.Are there examples of this in music? Or is it just something invented to test young pianists in exams?
 That's only in classical music. Jazzers play "pure" melodic minor both up and down.
 Thank you for this. I learned to write natural, harmonic, and melodic minor scales when I was a kid, but I never knew this explanation. It makes perfect sense!
 > they also have a starting pointIndeed. For anyone that is not sure about that, take a piano (like this virtual one[1]) and play a scale of all the white notes starting with C.The play a scale of all those same white notes, but starting with A.You should immediately notice that the second version is in a minor key (sounds "sadder").
 Awesome example! Folks, do this if this stuff is new to you, it's beautifully enlightening.
 Thanks, that's an explanation I haven't heard that way before, but that makes sense to me.Follow-up question: how are phase of the scale and the piece of music synchronized? When I think in terms of a wave, I could Fourier-Transform it into sines or in cosines, or any other phase-shifted variant (a * sin(nx + const.))?Is it always the first note of the piece of music that "anchors" the piece in its scale?Does that question even make sense? :-)
 Nice question, I think I can answer (although you'll soon notice that my understanding of this subject is limited as well)First of all, this is a mathematically undecidable problem, as in that there exist tunes that could be in multiple scales. The extreme example is a melody that consists of only a single note. Ridiculous, but as a fellow nerd I'm sure you can see how I could call a single note a melody. So we have to lose the phase metaphor here a little bit.Most songs "anchor" with the last note. If you really can't tell whether a song is in major or minor (songs in major are a bit happier, jollier; songs in minor a bit sadder and more melancholic), going for the very last note (or the last note of the chorus, if the song a chorus) is a very good bet. Find the core melody of the song, the thing everything is hung up on, the last note of that core melody is the ground note of the scale. This is a really safe bet.There are, of course, exceptions, and then I think you'll need to do some mathematical analysis to find out which key the notes in the melody match the best to. I'm not 100% sure how this is defined but I'm sure people have researched it.All that said, musicians don't really have this problem. After all, hardly any of this stuff was designed. The only genius piece of engineering in all this was the discovery that if you choose to 12 notes in an octave (and not 8 or 15 or whatever), you have a very versatile instrument that can play almost any melody at any pitch in a way that sounds pretty good to human ears (pretty good, not perfect, because of the all the ≈'s in the "Intervals" section of Eevee's article).But the rest was discovered, not designed, just by fooling around. The analysis came after the music.Musicians just start with a scale and then make the music they compose fit. This comes pretty natural to you with a bit of practice; most people have a pretty decent innate ability to hear which melodies "match" with a chord and which chords "match" with a previous chord. In all honesty, once you've understood things this far, I'd recommend fooling around with the easiest instrument or tool you know, rather than diving even deeper in to the mathematics of things :-)
 > The extreme example is a melody that consists of only a single note. Ridiculous, but as a fellow nerd I'm sure you can see how I could call a single note a melody.I got your single note melody right here:
 I was half expecting the One Note Samba (which is sort of the pathological case for the key-finding heuristic skrebbel describes).
 This gets pretty complicated and subjective.Strictly speaking, a "note" cannot define a key. To determine a key, you really need at least 2 intervals, wherein interval a somehow resolves to interval b. Keys are just a mapping of relationships; how does each of the 12 notes relate to each other? If you just play a C major chord, you aren't really playing in the key of CMaj, you're just playing a CMaj chord.For example: Play B+G, then C+G. This is (arguably) C major. The B note resolves into the C note. That's the 7th moving into the Tonic (#1). This is an example of a V-I resolution, the strongest possible. If you'd like more examples, look up "cadences". There are 'principles' for determining the key, but they should not be understood as proofs.I think you're also asking if/how to change keys. This is not only possible, but often highly encouraged. There are two extremely common key changes in pop music: 1) Up one step: This is incredibly common and can be heard in "I Will Always Love You" (album version, right after the 3 minute mark when she starts singing the chorus again). Once you start hearing this, you can't stop. It's all over the place. If we're in the key of C, we'll just play a D chord with a lot of confidence, typically after a G chord. 2) Minor to Major (or reverse, moving from sine to cosine in the analogy): This is much more subtle than the previous example. Typically you'll recognize this as a change in 'mood' or 'feeling'. You'll find a lot of Am-Em changes, then the chorus will be a lot of C-G changes. The tonic changes from Am to C, even though all the chords and notes being used are still the same (although the duration of said chords will probably be different).Most "classical" music moves around keys pretty frequently, in extreme cases multiple times within a measure. And then there are chords/sections<#sep#>-

#include <bits/stdc++.h>
using namespace std;

struct DSU {
    vector<int> parent;
    vector<int> size;
    vector<int> diameter;

    DSU(int n) : parent(n+1), size(n+1, 1), diameter(n+1, 0) {
        iota(parent.begin(), parent.end(), 0);
    }

    int find(int x) {
        if (parent[x] != x) {
            parent[x] = find(parent[x]);
        }
        return parent[x];
    }

    void unite(int x, int y) {
        x = find(x);
        y = find(y);
        if (x == y) return;
        if (size[x] < size[y]) swap(x, y);
        parent[y] = x;
        size[x] += size[y];
        diameter[x] = max({diameter[x], diameter[y], (diameter[x] + 1)/2 + (diameter[y] + 1)/2 + 1});
    }
};

int main() {
    ios_base::sync_with_stdio(false);
    cin.tie(nullptr);

    int n, m, q;
    cin >> n >> m >> q;

    vector<vector<int>> adj(n+1);
    DSU dsu(n);

    for (int i = 0; i < m; ++i) {
        int a, b;
        cin >> a >> b;
        adj[a].push_back(b);
        adj[b].push_back(a);
        dsu.unite(a, b);
    }

    vector<bool> visited(n+1, false);
    for (int i = 1; i <= n; ++i) {
        if (dsu.find(i) == i && !visited[i]) {
            queue<int> q;
            q.push(i);
            visited[i] = true;
            vector<int> component;
            while (!q.empty()) {
                int u = q.front();
                q.pop();
                component.push_back(u);
                for (int v : adj[u]) {
                    if (!visited[v]) {
                        visited[v] = true;
                        q.push(v);
                    }
                }
            }
            if (component.size() == 1) {
                dsu.diameter[i] = 0;
                continue;
            }
            int u = component[0], max_dist = 0;
            queue<pair<int, int>> q_bfs;
            vector<bool> visited_bfs(n+1, false);
            q_bfs.push({u, 0});
            visited_bfs[u] = true;
            while (!q_bfs.empty()) {
                auto [current, dist] = q_bfs.front();
                q_bfs.pop();
                if (dist > max_dist) {
                    max_dist = dist;
                    u = current;
                }
                for (int v : adj[current]) {
                    if (!visited_bfs[v]) {
                        visited_bfs[v] = true;
                        q_bfs.push({v, dist+1});
                    }
                }
            }
            max_dist = 0;
            visited_bfs.assign(n+1, false);
            q_bfs.push({u, 0});
            visited_bfs[u] = true;
            while (!q_bfs.empty()) {
                auto [current, dist] = q_bfs.front();
                q_bfs.pop();
                max_dist = max(max_dist, dist);
                for (int v : adj[current]) {
                    if (!visited_bfs[v]) {
                        visited_bfs[v] = true;
                        q_bfs.push({v, dist+1});
                    }
                }
            }
            dsu.diameter[i] = max_dist;
        }
    }

    while (q--) {
        int type;
        cin >> type;
        if (type == 1) {
            int x;
            cin >> x;
            cout << dsu.diameter[dsu.find(x)] << '\n';
        } else {
            int x, y;
            cin >> x >> y;
            dsu.unite(x, y);
        }
    }

    return 0;

</think>To find \( f(6) \) given the functional equation \( 2f(x) + 3f\left(\frac{2010}{x}\right) = 5x \) for all \( x > 0 \), we proceed as follows:

1. **Substitute \( x \) with \( \frac{2010}{x} \) in the original equation:**
   \[
   2f\left(\frac{2010}{x}\right) + 3f(x) = 5 \cdot \frac{2010}{x}
   \]
   This gives us a second equation:
   \[
   2f\left(\frac{2010}{x}\right) + 3f(x) = \frac{10050}{x}
   \]

2. **We now have a system of linear equations:**
   \[
   \begin{cases}
   2f(x) + 3f\left(\frac{2010}{x}\right) = 5x \\
   3f(x) + 2f\left(\frac{2010}{x}\right) = \frac{10050}{x}
   \end{cases}
   \]

3. **Eliminate \( f\left(\frac{2010}{x}\right) \) by multiplying the first equation by 3 and the second by 2:**
   \[
   \begin{cases}
   6f(x) + 9f\left(\frac{2010}{x}\right) = 15x \\
   6f(x) + 4f\left(\frac{2010}{x}\right) = \frac{20100}{x}
   \end{cases}
   \]

4. **Subtract the second equation from the first:**
   \[
   (6f(x) + 9f\left(\frac{2010}{x}\right)) - (6f(x) + 4f\left(\frac{2010}{x}\right)) = 15x - \frac{20100}{x}
   \]
   Simplifying, we get:
   \[
   5f\left(\frac{2010}{x}\right) = 15x - \frac{20100}{x}
   \]
   \[
   f\left(\frac{2010}{x}\right) = 3x - \frac{4020}{x}
   \]

5. **Substitute \( f\left(\frac{2010}{x}\right) \) back into the first equation:**
   \[
   2f(x) + 3\left(3x - \frac{4020}{x}\right) = 5x
   \]
   Simplifying, we get:
   \[
   2f(x) + 9x - \frac{12060}{x} = 5x
   \]
   \[
   2f(x) = 5x - 9x + \frac{12060}{x}
   \]
   \[
   2f(x) = -4x + \frac{12060}{x}
   \]
   \[
   f(x) = -2x + \frac{6030}{x}
   \]

6. **Evaluate \( f(6) \):**
   \[
   f(6) = -2 \cdot 6 + \frac{6030}{6}
   \]
   \[
   f(6) = -12 + 1005
   \]
   \[
   f(6) = 993
   \]

Thus, the value of \( f(6) \) is \(\boxed{993}\).<#sep#> check: f3=4, then f4 is 4^2 = 16 mod p which matches. So that makes sense.

Wait this is an important insight. If we can model each term f_i as equal to f_k^{e_i} mod p, where e_i is some exponent which can be calculated based on the recursion, then indeed f_n would be f_k^{e_n} mod p, and the equation to satisfy is f_k^{e_n} ≡ m mod p.

So then the problem reduces to computing e_n, the exponent for f_n in terms of f_k's exponent. Once that exponent is computed, find f_k such that (f_k)^{e_n} ≡ m mod p. However, solving this requires considering the exponents and the constraints modulo p and the multiplicative order of f_k.

But first, I need to find the exponent e_n. How to compute e_{i} ?

Wait the exponents might follow their own linear recurrence. Let's see. Since f_i = ( product_{j=1 to k} f_{i-j}^{b_j} ) mod p, and each f_{i-j} is f_k^{e_{i-j}}, so substituting in:

f_i = product_{j=1 to k} (f_k^{e_{i-j}})^{b_j} mod p. 

Which is f_k^{ sum_{j=1}^k ( b_j * e_{i-j} ) } mod p. Therefore, e_i = sum_{j=1}^k b_j * e_{i-j} modulo (p-1), since exponents can be modulo the multiplicative order of f_k. Wait, but exponentiation mod p involves Fermat's little theorem: since p is prime, any number a not divisible by p has a^(p-1)≡1 mod p. So exponents can be considered modulo (p-1)? 

Wait but the exponent here is e_i, which is an exponent of f_k. So to compute e_i, perhaps the exponents e_i follow a linear recurrence relation similar to the original f_i but using the exponents. Let me think:

Yes. Let's see:

Because when f_i is expressed as f_k^{e_i}, then the equation:

e_i = sum_{j=1}^k (b_j * e_{i-j}) mod (p-1). Wait, perhaps.

Wait the exponents must satisfy that relation, because f_i = product { (f_{i-j} )^{b_j} }, so:

the exponents need to add up appropriately. Specifically,

e_i equals the sum_{j=1}^k ( b_j * e_{i-j} ), because each term f_{i-j} is f_k^{e_{i-j}}, so when multiplied together with exponents b_j, the exponent of f_k in each term is b_j * e_{i-j}, so adding all those gives e_i.

Therefore, the exponents e_i follow a linear recurrence relation:

e_i = Σ_{j=1 to k} (b_j * e_{i-j}) 

for all i.

And importantly, the initial conditions for the exponents?

Wait the exponents e_1, e_2, ..., e_{k} ?

Wait perhaps the exponents for the first terms are set as per their definitions. Let me think again.

The exponent e_i is the exponent of f_k in the term f_i. Let's think step by step for i from 1 onward:

Yes, that must be the case. Similarly, e_2 must be 0 because f_2 is 1. All the way up. Except e_k, the exponent for f_k's term. 

Wait wait. Let me formalize the exponents' starting conditions:

Since the first (k-1) terms are 1, so:

e_1 = 0 (since f_1 = 1 must equal f_k^e_1 mod p, so 0 is the only exponent when f_k is non-zero, but the other terms are fixed as 1.)

Similarly:

e_2 = 0,

and so on up to e_{k-1} =0,

then e_k = 1, because f_k is the first term not set to 1. So f_k = f_k^1, so e_k =1.

Ah right! So the exponents for e_1 ... e_{k-1} are all zero, and e_k is 1. Then the recurrence relation for e_i is that for i >k:

e_i = b_1 * e_{i-1} + b_2*e_{i-2} + ... + b_k*e_{i-k}

So this forms a linear recurrence sequence for the exponents e_i.

Therefore, the exponents e_i are determined by their initial conditions and the recurrence. Then, once e_n is known, we can set up the equation (f_k)^{e_n} ≡ m mod p.

So our problem reduces to solving two parts:

1. Compute e_n, the exponent corresponding to the nth term in the exponent sequence, given the recurrence and the initial conditions.

2. For the equation f_k^{e_n} ≡ m mod p, find possible values of f_k, if any exist.

The first part requires computing e_n mod (p-1), because when exponentiating, exponents can be reduced modulo the Euler totient function (which for a prime p would be p-1). So, perhaps to compute e_n, even for large n (up to 1e9), we can model the linear recurrence efficiently, perhaps using matrix exponentiation or binary exponentiation on the recurrence relation.

The second part is to solve for f_k, given that (f_k)^e ≡ m mod p, where e is the exponent. This is the discrete logarithm problem in reverse—like a discrete root, or finding x such that x^e ≡ m mod p. Since p is a prime, we can do this by several methods:

- Compute if m ≡ 0 mod p: since the problem says m < p, so 1 ≤ m <p, so m is not zero.

- First, check if m and p are coprime. Because since mod p, and m is between 1 and p-1, they are.

We can then use the fact that in multiplicative group mod p (which has order p-1), the equation x^e ≡ m mod p has a solution if and only if m^{d} ≡ 1 mod p, where d is gcd(e, p-1). Wait, or perhaps more precisely, for the equation x^e ≡ m mod p to have a solution, m must be an e-th power residue. To check this, compute the greatest common divisor d of e and (p-1). Then m must be an element of order dividing (p-1)/d, so the multiplicative inverse here requires that m^((p-1)/d) ≡ 1 mod p.

Wait the condition for existence is that m^{(p-1)/d} ≡ 1 mod p, right?

So first, compute d = gcd(e_n, p-1). Compute g = gcd(e_n, p-1) = d. Then, if m^{ (p-1)/d } mod p is not 1, then there is no solution. 

If there is a solution, then the number of solutions is d, and we can find any<#sep#>Support optional properties
Currently all `{{...}}` placeholders in https://github.com/CODAIT/exchange-metadata-converter/tree/main/templates are considered to be required and therefore each placeholder input file must define them. Annotations would solve this issue. Investigate what it takes to support something like this, where `@annotation_key` serves as a hint to the processing engine and doesn't invalidate the YAML file because it is specified as a comment.

```
property: '{{value}}'         #  just a comment
another:
  property2: '{{value2}}'     # @optional and a comment
third_property:               #  comment only
fourth_property:
fifth_property: '{{value6}}'  # @annotation_only
```

<#sep#> Minor to Major (or reverse, moving from sine to cosine in the analogy): This is much more subtle than the previous example. Typically you'll recognize this as a change in 'mood' or 'feeling'. You'll find a lot of Am-Em changes, then the chorus will be a lot of C-G changes. The tonic changes from Am to C, even though all the chords and notes being used are still the same (although the duration of said chords will probably be different).Most "classical" music moves around keys pretty frequently, in extreme cases multiple times within a measure. And then there are chords/sections in which the key is debatable if not indiscernible (I would argue that the intro to Smashing Pumpkins "1979" is changing keys every 2 measures, but I think there's also a strong argument for it being in a single key)Thinking about keys in terms of phases is a good basic explanation, but ultimately phase is much easier to measure.  Torrent-of-ions is correct. Equal temperament is a compromise in tuning (assigning pitches to notes) which makes the most keys sound ok. The intervals aren't perfect, but close enough. If you tuned a piano for perfect intervals in one key, those pitches would be too much off for another key.
 Yes, equal temperament is basically a compromise of tuning methods. While it can help ensure that a piece which modulates into different keys is in tune (as close as possible), equal temperament cannot tell the key that is being played.

 > It completely obscures the relationship between the pitches, though.It doesn't actually obscure the relationship between the notes -- it makes them clearer. For example, I see the notes C, E, and G on some sheet music, maybe with some accidentals on some of those notes. I know that I'm therefore supposed to play a C triad. Now, there are multiple kinds of triads, but once I know I'm supposed to play a triad, it's easy to use context to pick out which one I need (major, minor, diminished, augmented -- usually one of the first two). If I were supposed to play a C# major triad, though, and the written notes were (C#-F-G#) as opposed to what they should be (C#-E#-G#) then that's confusing because it looks like I should be playing an arpeggiated sus4 of some kind. So the written nature of scales on the staff engenders an understanding of the relationship between the notes. Basically we write things the way we do so that the people reading the music can more efficiently pattern-match.> C major is identical to A minor, and I don’t understand why we need both.They're not identical.
