    # and giant steps.

    # Let's re-arrange the equation as:

    # We want x^e = m mod p.

    # Let’s choose K = (p-1) // gcd(e, p-1) = M?

    # Let me pick a parameter N such as N = ceil(p^(1/2)) or similar.

    # The BSGS method needs to solve for x in x^e = m mod p.

    # Let's rewrite this as:

    x^e mod p = m 

    equals:

    We can choose to compute all possible x^{e} mod p in baby steps and see.

    However, it's of order p which is not feasible.

    Alternative idea: Set x= g^k, where the equation becomes g^{e*k} = m mod p. 

    Then the equation is e*k ≡ log_g(m) mod (p-1).

    Which is a linear congruence equation.

    Thus, once we compute d= gcd(e, p-1), and m is in the solution space, then:

    e' = e/d

    M = (p-1)/d
    s = log_g of m
    
    So s must be divisible by d, so s = d *s'

    Then the equation e'*k = s' mod M → 

    k = (s' / e') mod M? 

    No, but e' has inverse mod M.

    k0 = s' * inverse(e', M) mod M

    So this gives us k0 mod M.

    Thus the first solution is k0 mod M.

    Thus x = g^{k_0} mod p.

    So this brings us back to needing to compute log_g(m).

    Thus, the main problem is to find the discrete logarithm of m.

    Hence, it is worth implementing a baby-step giant-step for that.

#include <bits/stdc++.h>
using namespace std;

vector<int> a;
vector<int> quartet_list;
vector<int> peak_valley_list;

int main() {
    ios_base::sync_with_stdio(false);
    cin.tie(0);
    int n, q;
    cin >> n >> q;
    a.resize(n);
    for (int i = 0; i < n; ++i)
        cin >> a[i];
    
    // Precompute quartet_list
    for (int i = 0; i <= n - 4; ++i) {
        bool valid = true;
        // Check triplet (i, i+1, i+2)
        if ((a[i] <= a[i+1] && a[i+1] <= a[i+2]) || (a[i] >= a[i+1] && a[i+1] >= a[i+2]))
            valid = false;
        if (valid) {
            // Check triplet (i, i+1, i+3)
            if ((a[i] <= a[i+1] && a[i+1] <= a[i+3]) || (a[i] >= a[i+1] && a[i+1] >= a[i+3]))
                valid = false;
        }
        if (valid) {
            // Check triplet (i, i+2, i+3)
            if ((a[i] <= a[i+2] && a[i+2] <= a[i+3]) || (a[i] >= a[i+2] && a[i+2] >= a[i+3]))
                valid = false;
        }
        if (valid) {
            // Check triplet (i+1, i+2, i+3)
            if ((a[i+1] <= a[i+2] && a[i+2] <= a[i+3]) || (a[i+1] >= a[i+2] && a[i+2] >= a[i+3]))
                valid = false;
        }
        if (valid)
            quartet_list.push_back(i);
    }
    
    // Precompute peak_valley_list
    for (int j = 1; j <= n - 2; ++j) {
        bool peak = (a[j-1] < a[j] && a[j] > a[j+1]);
        bool valley = (a[j-1] > a[j] && a[j] < a[j+1]);
        if (peak || valley)
            peak_valley_list.push_back(j);
    }
    
    while (q--) {
        int L, R;
        cin >> L >> R;
        L--; R--;
        int len = R - L + 1;
        if (len < 3) {
            cout << "0\n";
            continue;
        }
        bool found = false;
        if (len >= 4) {
            int start = L;
            int end = R - 3;
            if (start <= end) {
                auto it = lower_bound(quartet_list.begin(), quartet_list.end(), start);
                if (it != quartet_list.end() && *it <= end) {
                    int i = *it;
                    cout << "4\n";
                    cout << (i + 1) << ' ' << (i + 2) << ' ' << (i + 3) << ' ' << (i + 4) << '\n';
                    found = true;
                }
            }
        }
        if (!found) {
            int start_j = L + 1;
            int end_j = R - 1;
            if (start_j <= end_j) {
                auto jt = lower_bound(peak_valley_list.begin(), peak_valley_list.end(), start_j);
                if (jt != peak_valley_list.end() && *jt <= end_j) {
                    int j = *jt;
                    cout << "3\n";
                    cout << (j) << ' ' << (j + 1) << ' ' << (j + 2) << '\n';
                    found = true;
                }
            }
        }
        if (!found)
            cout << "0\n";
    }
    return 0;

</think>To solve the problem of finding the distance between stations B and G on a railway line divided into 10 sections by stations A, B, C, D, E, F, G, H, I, J, and K, we need to adhere to the given constraints:

1. The total distance from A to K is 56 km.
2. The distance between any two successive sections does not exceed 12 km.
3. The distance between any three successive sections is at least 17 km.

We need to find the distance between B and G, which involves the sections BC, CD, DE, EF, and FG.

### Step-by-Step Solution

1. **Identify the Constraints:**
   - Let the distances between successive stations be \( x_1, x_2, x_3, \ldots, x_{10} \).
   - The total distance is \( x_1 + x_2 + x_3 + \ldots + x_{10} = 56 \) km.
   - For any \( i \) from 1 to 9, \( x_i + x_{i+1} \leq 12 \) km.
   - For any \( i \) from 1 to 8, \( x_i + x_{i+1} + x_{i+2} \geq 17 \) km.

2. **Determine the Range of Each Section<#sep#>human: Today is Mashtali's birthday! He received a Hagh tree from Haj Davood as his birthday present!

A directed tree is called a Hagh tree iff: 

  * The length of the longest directed path in it is exactly n. 
  * Every vertex has at most three edges attached to it independent of their orientation. 
  * Let's call vertices u and v friends if one of them has a directed path to the other. For every pair of vertices u and v that are not friends, there should exist a vertex w that is friends with both u and v (a mutual friend). 



After opening his gift, Mashtali found out that the labels on the vertices were gone.

Immediately, he asked himself: how many different unlabeled Hagh trees are there? That is, how many possible trees could he have received as his birthday present?

At the first glance, the number of such trees seemed to be infinite since there was no limit on the number of vertices; but then he solved the problem and proved that there's a finite number of unlabeled Hagh trees!

Amazed by this fact, he shared the task with you so that you could enjoy solving it as well. Since the answer can be rather large he asked you to find the number of different unlabeled Hagh trees modulo 998244353.

Here two trees are considered different, if they are not isomorphic: if there is no way to map nodes of one tree to the second tree, so that edges are mapped to edges preserving the orientation.

Some examples for n = 2: 

<image>

Directed trees D and E are Hagh. C is not Hagh because it has a vertex with 4 edges attached to it. A and B are not Hagh because their longest directed paths are not equal to n. Also in B the leftmost and rightmost vertices are not friends neither do they have a mutual friend.
Input

The first line of input contains a single integer n (1 ≤ n ≤ 10^6).

Output

Print a single integer, the answer to Mashtali's task modulo 998244353.

Examples

Input


1


Output


5


Input


2


Output


31


Input


344031


Output


272040628

Note

All the five Hagh trees for n = 1:

Lab 2
Pneumatic Cylinder
Objective
Introduction to:
Ã¢â‚¬Â¢ Pneumatic Cylinders
Ã¢â‚¬Â¢ Propagation of Uncertainty
Introduction
A pneumatic cylinder converts energy stored in a pressurized gas into mechanical work. The high
pressure air exerts a large force on one side of the piston and the low pressure air exerts a smaller
force on the other side of the piston, resulting in a net force given by equation 1a (pressures are
gage pressure, not absolute pressure). This force causes the piston (and the attached rod) to extend
or retract, depending on the direction of the net force. If the low pressure side is at atmospheric
pressure, then equation 1a becomes 1b, which is the case in this lab.
Ã°Ââ€˜Â­ = Ã°Ââ€˜Â·Ã°Ââ€™â€žÃ°Ââ€™â€šÃ°Ââ€™â€˜ Ã¢Ë†â€” Ã°Ââ€˜Â¨Ã°Ââ€™â€žÃ°Ââ€™â€šÃ°Ââ€™â€˜ Ã¢Ë†â€™ Ã°Ââ€˜Â·Ã°Ââ€™â€œÃ°Ââ€™ÂÃ°Ââ€™â€¦ Ã¢Ë†â€” Ã°Ââ€˜Â¨Ã°Ââ€™â€œÃ°Ââ€™ÂÃ°Ââ€™â€¦
Ã°Ââ€˜Â­=Ã°Ââ€˜Â·Ã¢Ë†â€”Ã°Ââ€˜Â¨
(1a)
(1b)
The terminology used in pneumatic cylinders refers to the larger area (left side of the piston in
figure 1) as the Ã¢â‚¬Å“capÃ¢â‚¬Â side of the piston and the smaller piston area (right side of the piston in figure
1) as the Ã¢â‚¬Å“rodÃ¢â‚¬Â side of the piston. Notice that the rod side area is smaller than the cap side area.
Also, note that Arod in this case does not refer to the area of the rod, but rather, the area of the rod
side of the piston. Figure 1 depicts the anatomy of a typical pneumatic cylinder. These devices
are also known as linear actuators because they produce linear motion.
Figure 1: Anatomy of a typical air cylinder
The advantages of using a pneumatic cylinder:
Ã¢â‚¬Â¢ Air cylinders are relatively quiet in operation
Ã¢â‚¬Â¢ Air cylinders are clean Ã¢â‚¬â€œ there is no hazardous fluid that can leak into the surroundings
Ã¢â‚¬Â¢ Initial cost is low
The disadvantages of using a pneumatic cylinder:
Ã¢â‚¬Â¢ Can lack precision if the load on the cylinder is compressing the gas as the cylinder actuates
(i.e. extending or retracting in vertical position)
Ã¢â‚¬Â¢ Relatively poor speed control
Ã¢â‚¬Â¢ The long term cost of the increased energy required run the air compressor
Single-acting cylinders (SAC) – Air cylinders which have only one air supply to push the piston
only one way. A SAC type air cylinder usually has an internal compression spring resetting the
piston after actuation.
Double-acting cylinders (DAC) – Air cylinders that have air supply ports on both ends. This type
of air cylinder is more controllable (position and speed) by controlling the differential pressure on
both air supply ports.
One-sided cylinder Ã<#sep#>-

#include <bits/stdc++.h>
using namespace std;

void findBridges(int u, int parent, int &time, vector<int>& disc, vector<int>& low, vector<bool>& visited, const vector<vector<int>>& adj, set<pair<int, int>>& bridges) {
    visited[u] = true;
    disc[u] = low[u] = time++;
    for (int v : adj[u]) {
        if (v == parent) continue;
        if (!visited[v]) {
            findBridges(v, u, time, disc, low, visited, adj, bridges);
            low[u] = min(low[u], low[v]);
            if (low[v] > disc[u]) {
                bridges.insert({min(u, v), max(u, v)});
            }
        } else {
            low[u] = min(low[u], disc[v]);
        }
    }
}

int main() {
    ios_base::sync_with_stdio(false);
    cin.tie(nullptr);

    int n, m;
    cin >> n >> m;
    vector<vector<int>> adj(n + 1);
    for (int i = 0; i < m; ++i) {
        int a, b;
        cin >> a >> b;
        adj[a].push_back(b);
        adj[b].push_back(a);
    }

    set<pair<int, int>> bridges;
    vector<int> disc(n + 1), low(n + 1);
    vector<bool> visited(n + 1, false);
    int time = 0;

    for (int u = 1; u <= n; ++u) {
        if (!visited[u]) {
            findBridges(u, -1, time, disc, low, visited, adj, bridges);
        }
    }

    vector<int> comp(n + 1, -1);
    int current_comp = 0;
    for (int u = 1; u <= n; ++u) {
        if (comp[u] == -1) {
            queue<int> q;
            q.push(u);
            comp[u] = current_comp;
            while (!q.empty()) {
                int v = q.front();
                q.pop();
                for (int w : adj[v]) {
                    if (comp[w] == -1 && !bridges.count({min(v, w), max(v, w)})) {
                        comp[w] = current_comp;
                        q.push(w);
                    }
                }
            }
            current_comp++;
        }
    }

    if (current_comp == 1) {
        cout << 0 << endl;
        return 0;
    }

    vector<unordered_set<int>> bridge_tree(current_comp);
    for (auto& bridge : bridges) {
        int u = bridge.first;
        int v = bridge.second;
        int cu = comp[u];
        int cv = comp[v];
        bridge_tree[cu].insert(cv);
        bridge_tree[cv].insert(cu);
    }

    vector<int> leaves;
    for (int i = 0; i < current_comp; ++i) {
        if (bridge_tree[i].size() == 1) {
            leaves.push_back(i);
        }
    }

    if (leaves.empty()) {
        cout << 0 << endl;
        return 0;
    }

    vector<vector<int>> comp_nodes(current_comp);
    for (int u = 1; u <= n; ++u) {
        comp_nodes[comp[u]].push_back(u);
    }

    vector<int> reps;
    for (int leaf_comp : leaves) {
        reps.push_back(comp_nodes[leaf_comp][0]);
    }

    int t = (reps.size() + 1) / 2;
    cout << t << endl;

    for (int i = 0; i < t; ++i) {
        int j = (i + t) % reps.size();
        cout << reps[i] << " " << reps[j] << endl;
    }

    return 0;

<#sep#>â‚¬Â¢ Relatively poor speed control
Ã¢â‚¬Â¢ The long term cost of the increased energy required run the air compressor
Single-acting cylinders (SAC) – Air cylinders which have only one air supply to push the piston
only one way. A SAC type air cylinder usually has an internal compression spring resetting the
piston after actuation.
Double-acting cylinders (DAC) – Air cylinders that have air supply ports on both ends. This type
of air cylinder is more controllable (position and speed) by controlling the differential pressure on
both air supply ports.
One-sided cylinder Ã¢â‚¬â€œ A cylinder with a rod extending out in only one direction. This is what is
being used in this lab.
Two-sided cylinder Ã¢â‚¬â€œ A cylinder with a rod extending out of each side. Typically the rod diameter
is equal on both sides, resulting in simplified analysis because both sides of the piston will have
the same area.
Bore Ã¢â‚¬â€œ Refers to the diameter of the cylinder barrel in which the piston travels. This dimension
will be used to calculate the piston area.
Position Sensors (Switches) Ã¢â‚¬â€œ The cylinderÃ¢â‚¬â„¢s speed and position can be controlled by having a
magnetized piston traveling inside the cylinder barrel. The position of the magnetized barrel can
be sensed by position sensors (switches) attached to the outside of the cylinder barrel via the Hall
effect.

Why do we use gage pressure instead of absolute pressure in the above analysis?
The absolute pressure ultimately is what determines the force. Consider<#sep#>-

#include <bits/stdc++.h>
using namespace std;

struct DSU {
    vector<int> parent;
    vector<int> max_val;
    vector<int> max_node;

    DSU(int n, const vector<int>& p) {
        parent.resize(n + 1);
        max_val.resize(n + 1);
        max_node.resize(n + 1);
        for (int i = 1; i <= n; ++i) {
            parent[i] = i;
            max_val[i] = p[i - 1];
            max_node[i] = i;
        }
    }

    int find(int u) {
        if (parent[u] != u) {
            parent[u] = find(parent[u]);
        }
        return parent[u];
    }

    void unite(int u, int v) {
        u = find(u);
        v = find(v);
        if (u == v) return;
        if (max_val[u] > max_val[v]) {
            parent[v] = u;
        } else if (max_val[v] > max_val[u]) {
            parent[u] = v;
        } else {
            if (u < v) {
                parent[v] = u;
            } else {
                parent[u] = v;
            }
        }
        int new_max = max(max_val[u], max_val[v]);
        int new_node = (max_val[u] == new_max) ? max_node[u] : max_node[v];
        max_val[find(u)] = new_max;
        max_node[find(u)] = new_node;
    }

    pair<int, int> get_max(int u) {
        u = find(u);
        return {max_val[u], max_node[u]};
    }
};

int main() {
    ios::sync_with_stdio(false);
    cin.tie(nullptr);

    int n, m, q;
    cin >> n >> m >> q;
    vector<int> p(n);
    for (int i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
        cin >> p[i];
    }
    vector<pair<int, int>> edges(m + 1); // 1-based
    for (int i = 1; i <= m; ++i) {
        cin >> edges[i].first >> edges[i].second;
    }
    vector<pair<char, int>> queries(q);
    vector<bool> edge_deleted(m + 1, false);
    for (int i = 0; i < q; ++i) {
        char type;
        int val;
        cin >> type >> val;
        queries[i] = {type, val};
        if (type == '2') {
            edge_deleted[val] = true;
        }
    }

    DSU dsu(n, p);
    // Add edges not deleted in any query
    for (int i = 1; i <= m; ++i) {
        if (!edge_deleted[i]) {
            dsu.unite(edges[i].first, edges[i].second);
        }
    }

    vector<int> ans;
    vector<int> current_p = p;

    for (int i = q - 1; i >= 0; --i) {
        auto [type, val] = queries[i];
        if (type == '2') {
            int a = edges[val].first;
            int b = edges[val].second;
            dsu.unite(a, b);
        } else {
            int v = val;
            auto [max_v, u] = dsu.get_max(v);
            ans.push_back(max_v);
            if (max_v > 0) {
                current_p[u - 1] = 0;
                // Update the DSU's max_val and max_node
                dsu.max_val[u] = 0;
                // Propagate the change by reconnecting
                // This part is a simplification and may not work for all cases
                // In practice, a more sophisticated DSU with ability to update values is needed
                dsu.parent[u] = u;
                dsu.max_val[u] = 0;
                dsu.max_node[u] = u;
                for (int neighbor : {edges[val].first, edges[val].second}) {
                    if (neighbor == u) continue;
                    if (dsu.find(neighbor) == u) continue;
                    dsu.unite(u, neighbor);
                }
            }
        }
    }

    reverse(ans.begin(), ans.end());
    for (int a : ans) {
        cout << a << '\n';
    }

    return 0;
}<#sep#>Creative Math and Pepemath are playing a game on the unit circle. Initially, Creative Math places 2022 frogs on the circumference. Pepemath wins if, at the end of any turn, the center of mass of the 2022 frogs is less than $\frac{1}{1000}$ unit distance from the origin. If Pepemath cannot win within 69420 turns, Creative Math wins. Who has a winning strategy?

Okay, let's try to figure out this problem. So, we have Creative Math and Pepemath playing a game on a unit circle with 2022 frogs. The goal is to determine who has a winning strategy. Let me break down the problem step by step.

First, the setup: Initially, Creative Math places 2022 frogs on the circumference of the unit circle. Each turn, Pepemath does two things: removes one frog and makes another frog jump 60 degrees clockwise. Then, Creative Math adds a new frog to the circumference. The victory condition for Pepemath is that after any turn, the center of mass (CM) of all 2022 frogs is less than 1/1000 units from the origin. If Pepemath can't achieve this within 69,420 turns, Creative Math wins.

Hmm. So, we need to analyze if Pepemath can manipulate the frogs' positions such that the center of mass gets arbitrarily close to the origin, or if Creative Math can prevent that indefinitely.

Let me recall that the center of mass of points on a unit circle can be represented using complex numbers. Each frog's position is a complex number of modulus 1. The center of mass is the average of all these complex numbers.

So, if we denote the positions of the frogs as \( z_1, z_2, \ldots, z_{2022} \), then the center of mass \( C \) is:

\( C = \frac{1}{2022} \sum_{k=1}^{2022} z_k \)

Pepemath wants to make \( |C| < \frac{1}{1000} \).

Each turn, Pepemath removes a frog (say, at position \( z_j \)) and makes another frog (say, at position \( z_i \)) jump 60 degrees clockwise. So, after the jump, the frog that was at \( z_i \) is now at \( z_i \cdot e^{-i\pi/3} \). Then, Creative Math adds a new frog, which could be anywhere on the circumference.

So, each turn involves replacing two frogs: removing one, modifying another's position, and adding a new one. So, total number of frogs remains 2022 each turn.

Let me consider each turn's effect on the center of mass. Let me model the positions as complex numbers. Let me track the sum \( S = \sum_{k=1}^{2022} z_k \), so \( C = S / 2022 \).

Each turn:

1. Pepemath removes a frog at position \( z_j \), so the sum becomes \( S - z_j \).

2. Then, Pepemath makes another frog (at position \( z_i \)) jump 60 degrees clockwise. So, that frog's position becomes \( z_i \cdot e^{-i\pi/3} \). Therefore, the sum becomes \( S - z_j - z_i + z_i e^{-i\pi/3} = S - z_j - z_i (1 - e^{-i\pi/3}) \).

3. Then, Creative Math adds a new frog at some position \( z_{\text{new}} \). So, the sum becomes \( S' = S - z_j - z_i (1 - e^{-i\pi/3}) + z_{\text{new}} \).

Therefore, the change in the sum is:

\( \Delta S = -z_j - z_i (1 - e^{-i\pi/3}) + z_{\text{new}} \).

And the new center of mass is \( S' / 2022 \).

Pepemath's goal is to make \( |S'| / 2022 < 1/1000 \), i.e., \( |S'| < 2022 / 1000 \approx 2.022 \).

So, the key is to analyze whether Pepemath can drive \( |S| \) down below 2.022 through these operations. If so, Pepemath can win; otherwise, Creative Math can keep the center of mass away.

\( \Delta S = -z_j + (e^{-i\pi/3} - 1) z_i + z_{\text{new}} \).

Appendix:
Why do we use gage pressure instead of absolute pressure in the above analysis?
The absolute pressure ultimately is what determines the force. Consider all the forces acting on
the piston-rod assembly in the direction of motion:
1) the absolute pressure in the cap side acting on the cap side area
2) the absolute pressure in the rod side acting on the rod side area
3) the absolute pressure (atmospheric) acting on the tip of the rod
4) friction between the cylinder wall and the piston.
Conveniently, the friction force goes to zero as the piston velocity goes to zero, leaving the other
3 forces. The resulting force is given below

#include <iostream>
#include <vector>
#include <tuple>
#include <algorithm>

using namespace std;

struct SegNode {
    long long sum_v;
    long long sum_w;
    int a;
    int w;
    int v;
    int l, r;
    int start, end;
};

vector<SegNode> seg;

int build_segment_tree(int start, int end, const vector<tuple<int, int, int>>& data) {
    int node_idx = seg.size();
    seg.push_back(SegNode());
    auto& node = seg.back();
    node.start = start;
    node.end = end;
    if (start == end) {
        int a = get<0>(data[start]);
        int w = get<1>(data[start]);
        int v = get<2>(data[start]);
        node.sum_v = (long long)a * v;
        node.sum_w = (long long)a * w;
        node.a = a;
        node.w = w;
        node.v = v;
        node.l = node.r = -1;
    } else {
        int mid = (start + end) / 2;
        node.l = build_segment_tree(start, mid, data);
        node.r = build_segment_tree(mid + 1, end, data);
        node.sum_v = seg[node.l].sum_v + seg[node.r].sum_v;
        node.sum_w = seg[node.l].sum_w + seg[node.r].sum_w;
        node.a = 0;
        node.w = 0;
        node.v = 0;
    }
    return node_idx;
}

void update(int node_idx, int pos, int new_a) {
    SegNode& node = seg[node_idx];
    if (node.start == node.end) {
        node.a = new_a;
        node.sum_v = (long long)new_a * node.v;
        node.sum_w = (long long)new_a * node.w;
    } else {
        if (pos <= seg[node.l].end) {
            update(node.l, pos, new_a);
        } else {
            update(node.r, pos, new_a);
        }
        node.sum_v = seg[node.l].sum_v + seg[node.r].sum_v;
        node.sum_w = seg[node.l].sum_w + seg[node.r].sum_w;
    }
}

int get_a(int node_idx, int pos) {
    SegNode& node = seg[node_idx];
    if (node.start == node.end) {
        return node.a;
    } else {
        if (pos <= seg[node.l].end) {
            return get_a(node.l, pos);
        } else {
            return get_a(node.r, pos);
        }
    }
}

long long query_max(int node_idx, long long& capacity) {
    SegNode& node = seg[node_idx];
    if (node.sum_w <= capacity) {
        capacity -= node.sum_w;
        return node.sum_v;
    }
    if (node.start == node.end) {
        long long take = min((long long)node.a, capacity / node.w);
        long long res = take * node.v;
        capacity -= take * node.w;
        return res;
    }
    long long res = 0;
    res += query_max(node.l, capacity);
    res += query_max(node.r, capacity);
    return res;
}

int main() {
    ios_base::sync_with_stdio(false);
    cin.tie(nullptr);
    int n, q;
    cin >> n >> q;
    vector<tuple<int, int, int, int>> sorted_types; // v, w, a, original_d
    for (int d = 1; d <= n; d++) {
        int a, w, v;
        cin >> a >> w >> v;
        sorted_types.emplace_back(v, w, a, d);
    }
    sort(sorted_types.begin(), sorted_types.end(), [](const auto& a, const auto& b) {
        if (get<0>(a) != get<0>(b)) return get<0>(a) > get<0>(b);
        return get<1>(a) < get<1>(b);
    });
    vector<int> pos(n + 1);
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        int d = get<3>(sorted_types[i]);
        pos[d] = i;
    }
    vector<tuple<int, int, int>> data(n);
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        data[i] = make_tuple(get<2>(sorted_types[i]), get<1>(sorted_types[i]), get<0>(sorted_types[i]));
    }
    seg.reserve(4 * n);
    build_segment_tree(0, n - 1, data);
    while (q--) {
        int t;
        cin >> t;
        if (t == 1 || t == 2) {
            int k, d;
            cin >> k >> d;
            int p = pos[d];
            int current_a = get_a(0, p);
            if (t == 1) {
                current_a += k;
            } else {
                current_a -= k;
            }
            update(0, p, current_a);
        } else {
            long long c;
            cin >> c;
            long long capacity = c;
            long long total = query_max(0, capacity);
            cout << total << '\n';
        }
    }
    return 0;

So edges must be between any two nodes in some direction, but as long as no direction forms paths longer than 1. So for example, edges can be from A to B and B to A? No, because that would form a cycle. But directed tree can't have cycles, so that's impossible. 

Wait the structure must be a tree (undirected edges are a tree), So with three nodes forming a chain like A-B-C would be an undirected path, which as directed edges can be directed in a single direction. So perhaps A→B and B→C. This forms a path of length 2, which would be invalid for n=1. 

Thus, for three nodes, to have all paths of length at most 1, the three nodes must be connected in a shape without paths of length more than 1. 

The only way this is possible is: three nodes forming a "T" shape where they don't have edges that form a path. Like:

Edges between A and B and A and C in both directions? No, the three nodes' undirected edges would form a structure that is a tree (so three edges? No, a tree has exactly n-1 edges for n nodes). So with three nodes: two edges must be present. 

Wait a tree with three nodes has two edges. So, three nodes arranged as a root with two children: edges from A to B and A to C. That’s a total of two edges, forming a star-shaped structure. The path lengths are all at most 1 (from root to children). So that would be allowed. The third condition holds as discussed. 

Another option would be edges from A to B and B to C? But that creates a path of length 2 between A and C, which is invalid for n=1. 

So three-node trees for n=1 must be a star where the root has two edges, and other nodes have no outgoing edges. 

Wait other direction? For example, edges from B to A<#sep#>Days are still returned in time points are given for a vector quantity on materials, e.g. burnup
Fix in #2 did not take in to account quantities like `burnup` and `volume` that do not return arrays for isotope quantities.
If the user specifies one of these quantities from a depleted material, the time points are still returned.

<#sep#># AVERAGE

Examples

1. A batsman has a certain average runs for 16 innings. In the 17th inning he made a score of 85 runs thereby his average is increased by 3. What is his average after 17th inning?Examples

Sol: The average for 17th inning has been increased by 3.
The total increase in the runs for 17th inning = 17 × 3
= 51
But the batsman scores 85.
Average runs in his 16th innings = 85 – 51
= 34.
Hence the average of runs after 17th innings = 34  + 3
= 37

2.  A man has 7 children. When their average age was 12 years, the child who was 6 years of age, died. What was the average age of surviving children 5 years after the death of the above child?
Sol: Average age of 7 children  = 12 years
Total age of 6 children  =  12 × 7 = 84 years
Total age of 6 children after the death of a child aged 6 years = 84 – 6 = 78
Hence   the average age of the surviving children =
After 5 yrs. = 13 + 5 = 18 yrs.

3.  If the weights of 5 students of a class are 49.6 kg, 39.8 kg, 45.2 kg and 24.6 kg respectively then what is their average weight?
Sol: Total weight of 5 students = 49.6 + 39.8 + 40.8 + 45.2 + 24.6
= 200 kg.
∴    Their average weight =
= 40 kg.

4.  The average temperature for Monday, Tuesday and Wednesday was 36oC. The average temperature for Tuesday, Wednesday and Thursday was. If the temperature for Thursday was 37oC, what was the temperature on Monday?
Sol:   Average temperature for Monday, Tuesday and Wednesday = 36oC
Total temperature for Monday, Tuesday and Wednesday = 36 × 3
= 108oC
∴   Average temperature for Tuesday, Wednesday and Thursday = 38oC
∴    Total temperature for Tuesday, Wednesday and Thursday = 38 × 3
= 114oC
∴    Total temperature for Tuesday and Wednesday only = 114 – 37
=  77oC
∴    Temperature for  Monday only = 108 – 77
= 31oC

5.  A train covers the first 16 km at a speed of 20 km per hour another 20 km at 40 km per hour and the last 10 km at 15 km per hour. Find the average speed for the entire journey.

6.  A vehicle travels from A to B at the speed of 40 km/hr, but from B to A at the speed of 60km/hr. what is its average speed during the whole journey?
Sol: Let the distance from A to B be x km

7.  The average age of a class of 40 boys is 16.95 years. A new boy joins the class and the average age now is 17 years. What is the age of the new boy?
Sol: The average age of 40 boys  = 16.95 years
Total are of 40 boys  = 16.95 × 40
= 678 years
The average age of 41 boys  = 17 years
Total age of 41 boys  = 17 × 41
= 697 years
Age of the new boy  = 697 – 678
= 19 years

The average of the given quantities is calculated by dividing the sum of the quantities by their number.

If the average of ‘x’ quantities is ‘a’ and average of ‘y’ quantities is ‘b’, then average of x and y is

Weighted Average
Let x1, x2, x3, …..xn be the quantities and w1, w2, w3, ….wn be the weights attached to them then Weighted Average

SOLVED EXAMPLES

Example 1. A cricketer has completed 18 innings and his average is 26.5 runs. How many runs must he make in his next innings so as to raise his average to 27?
(1) 36           (2)46            (3)38            (4) 40            (5) None of these
Explanation:
(1)   Formula = n(y – x) + y
n = 18
x = 265
y = 27
= 18(27 – 26.5) + 27
= 9 + 7
= 36

Example 2. The average of 13 numbers is 30. The average of 1st 7 of these numbers is 32 and the last 7 of these numbers is 22. Find the 7th number.
(1) 15         (2) 16          (3) 12          (4) 20          (5) None of these
Explanation:
(3)    Total number = 13 × 30 = 390
1st 7 numbers = 7 × 32 = 224
Last 7 number = 7 × 22 = 154
7th number = 390 – (224 + 154)
= 12

Example 3. The average of marks obtained by 65 candidates in a certain examination is 25. If the average marks of passed candidates is 27 and that of the failed candidates is 14, what is the no. of candidates who passed the examination?
(1) 53            (2) 64            (3) 55            (4) 70            (5) None of these
Explanation:
(3)  Passed candidates = x
Failed candidates = 65 – x
Total marks ∴   25  ×  65 = 27x + (65 – x)14
⇒ 1625 = 27x + 910 – 14x
⇒ 13x = 715
⇒ x = 55

Example 4. In a class there are 30 students whose average is decreased by6 months, when 4 students aged 16, 17, 18 and 19 years respectively are replaced by the some no. of students. Find the average of the new students.
(1) 16.25               (2) 15.25               (3) 16               (4) 15.75               (5) None of these

Example 5. The average age of 11 persons in a committee is increased by 2 years when three men aged 32 years, 33 years and 34 years are substituted by three women. Find the average age of these three women.
(1) 40 1/3        (2) 40          (3) 41 1/3        (4) 41 years          (5) None of these

Example 6. The average of 40 numbers is 405. If each of the numbers is divided by 15. Find the average of new set of numbers.
(1) 27          (2) 28          (3) 21          (4) 26          (5) None of these

Explanation:

405/15 = 27

Example 7. The average age of 80 boys in a class is 15. The average age of a group of 15 boys in the class is 16 and the average age of another 25 boys in the class is 14. What is the average age of remaining boys in the class. (approx.)
(1) 20          (2) 15          (3) 18          (4) 21          (5) None of these
Explanation:
(2)    Remaining boys average age = x
⇒ 15 × 16 + 25 × 14 + 40x = 80 × 15
⇒ 240 + 350 + 40x = 1200
⇒ 40x = 1200 – 590
x = 610/40
x = 15. 25

Example 8. A batsman in his 12th innings makes a score of 63 runs and there by increases his<#sep#>-

#include <bits/stdc++.h>

using namespace std;

const int INF = 1e9;

struct Point {
    int x, y;
    Point(int x = 0, int y = 0) : x(x), y(y) {}

    bool operator<(const Point& other) const {
        if (x != other.x) return x < other.x;
        return y < other.y;
    }
};

int main() {
    int n, m, k;
    cin >> n >> m >> k;

    vector<vector<int>> a(n, vector<int>(m));
    for (int i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
        for (int j = 0; j < m; ++j) {
            cin >> a[i][j];
        }
    }

    vector<Point> points;
    for (int i = 0; i < k; ++i) {
        int x, y;
        cin >> x >> y;
        x--; y--;
        points.emplace_back(x, y);
    }

    sort(points.begin(), points.end());
    int best_sum = INF;
    vector<Point> best_cells;

    do {
        int current_sum = 0;
        vector<vector<bool>> current_tree(n, vector<bool>(m, false));
        Point first = points[0];
        current_tree[first.x][first.y] = true;
        current_sum = a[first.x][first.y];

        bool valid = true;
        for (int step = 1; step < k; ++step) {
            Point target = points[step];

            vector<vector<int>> dist(n, vector<int>(m, INF));
            vector<vector<Point>> parent(n, vector<Point>(m, Point(-1, -1)));
            priority_queue<tuple<int, int, int>, vector<tuple<int, int, int>>, greater<tuple<int, int, int>>> pq;

            for (int x = 0; x < n; ++x) {
                for (int y = 0; y < m; ++y) {
                    if (current_tree[x][y]) {
                        dist[x][y] = 0;
                        pq.emplace(0, x, y);
                    }
                }
            }

            bool found = false;
            while (!pq.empty()) {
                auto [cost, x, y] = pq.top();
                pq.pop();

                if (x == target.x && y == target.y) {
                    found = true;
                    break;
                }

                if (cost > dist[x][y]) continue;

                const int dx[] = {-1, 1, 0, 0};
                const int dy[] = {0, 0, -1, 1};
                for (int d = 0; d < 4; ++d) {
                    int nx = x + dx[d];
                    int ny = y + dy[d];
                    if (nx < 0 || nx >= n || ny < 0 || ny >= m) continue;

                    int new_cost = cost + (current_tree[nx][ny] ? 0 : a[nx][ny]);
                    if (new_cost < dist[nx][ny]) {
                        dist[nx][ny] = new_cost;
                        parent[nx][ny] = Point(x, y);
                        pq.emplace(new_cost, nx, ny);
                    }
                }
            }

            if (!found) {
                valid = false;
                break;
            }

            vector<Point> path;
            Point current(target.x, target.y);
            while (true) {
                path.push_back(current);
                if (current_tree[current.x][current.y]) break;
                Point p = parent[current.x][current.y];
                if (p.x == -1 && p.y == -1) break;
                current = p;
            }

            for (int i = 0; i < path.size() - 1; ++i) {
                auto& cell = path[i];
                if (!current_tree[cell.x][cell.y]) {
                    current_tree[cell.x][cell.y] = true;
                    current_sum += a[cell.x][cell.y];
                }
            }
        }

        if (valid && current_sum < best_sum) {
            best_sum = current_sum;
            best_cells.clear();
            for (int x = 0; x < n; ++x) {
                for (int y = 0; y < m; ++y) {
                    if (current_tree[x][y]) {
                        best_cells.emplace_back(x, y);
                    }
                }
            }
        }

    } while (next_permutation(points.begin(), points.end()));

    cout << best_sum << endl;

    vector<vector<char>> grid(n, vector<char>(m, '.'));
    for (auto& p : best_cells) {
        grid[p.x][p.y] = 'X';
    }

    for (int i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
        for (int j = 0; j < m; ++j) {
            cout << grid[i][j];
        }
        cout << endl;
    }

    return 0;
}<#sep#> if we have \( - z_i (1 - e^{-i\pi/3}) \), and \( 1 - e^{-iπ/3} = e^{iπ/3} \), then this is \( - z_i e^{iπ/3} \). So, subtract a vector that is \( z_i \) rotated by 60 degrees counterclockwise. So, the effect is equivalent to removing \( z_i \) and adding \( z_i e^{-iπ/3} \), which is a 60 degrees clockwise rotation. So, the net effect on the sum is \( - z_i + z_i e^{-iπ/3} = - z_i (1 - e^{-iπ/3}) = - z_i e^{iπ/3} \). So, in terms of vectors, this is equivalent to subtracting a vector of the same length as \( z_i \), rotated 60 degrees counterclockwise.

Therefore, the total change \( \Delta S = -z_j - z_i e^{iπ/3} + z_{\text{new}} \).

So, Pepemath can choose which \( z_j \) to remove and which \( z_i \) to rotate. Then, Creative Math adds \( z_{\text{new}} \), a unit vector, in any direction.

Now, the question is, can Pepemath choose \( z_j \) and \( z_i \) such that, regardless of Creative Math's \( z_{\text{new}} \), the sum \( S' = S + \Delta S \) has a smaller magnitude than before?

Alternatively, can Pepemath ensure that even if Creative Math adds \( z_{\text{new}} \) in the direction that maximizes |S'|, the magnitude still decreases?

This is similar to a game where two players alternately modify a vector, one trying to minimize its norm, the other trying to maximize it.

To analyze this, let's consider the maximum possible |S'| after a turn, given Pepemath's moves. If Pepemath can choose \( z_j \) and \( z_i \) such that even when Creative Math adds \( z_{\text{new}} \) optimally, |S'| is less than |S| by some fixed amount each turn, then eventually |S| would be driven below 2.022. Otherwise, if Creative Math can always respond by adding a frog that keeps |S| from decreasing enough, then Creative Math can win.

Alternatively, if Pepemath can compensate for the addition of the new frog by removing and rotating existing frogs in such a way that the net effect is a reduction in |S|.

Let me consider the triangle inequality. The change in S is \( \Delta S = -z_j - z_i e^{i\pi/3} + z_{\text{new}} \). Therefore, the new sum is:

\( S' = S - z_j - z_i e^{i\pi/3} + z_{\text{new}} \).

To bound |S'|, we can use the triangle inequality:

\( |S'| \leq |S - z_j - z_i e^{i\pi/3}| + |z_{\text{new}}| \).

But since |z_new| = 1, and Creative Math will choose z_new to maximize |S'|, which would be in the direction of \( S - z_j - z_i e^{i\pi/3} \). Therefore, the maximum |S'| is |S - z_j - z_i e^{i\pi/3}| + 1.

But if Pepemath can choose z_j and z_i such that |S - z_j - z_i e^{i\pi/3}| is minimized, then |S'| would be minimized. Wait, but since Creative Math is trying to maximize |S'|, they would add z_new in the direction of \( S - z_j - z_i e^{i\pi/3} \), so:

\( |S'| = |S - z_j - z_i e^{i\pi/3} + z_{\text{new}}| \leq |S - z_j - z_i e^{i\pi/3}| + |z_{\text{new}}| \), but equality occurs when z_new is in the same direction as \( S - z_j - z_i e^{i\pi/3} \). Therefore, Creative Math can make \( |S'| = |S - z_j - z_i e^{i\pi/3}| + 1 \).

Therefore, the maximum possible |S'| is |S - z_j - z_i e^{i\pi/3}| + 1. Therefore, Pepemath wants to choose z_j and z_i to minimize this quantity. So, the minimal possible maximum |S'| is the minimum over all possible z_j, z_i of (|S - z_j - z_i e^{iπ/3}| + 1). So, the question is, given S, can Pepemath choose z_j and z_i such that this expression is less than |S|, leading to a net decrease.

Alternatively, is there a choice of z_j and z_i such that |S - z_j - z_i e^{iπ/3}| + 1 ≤ |S| - ε for some ε > 0? If so, then Pepemath can drive |S| down over time.

Alternatively, if regardless of Pepemath's choices, Creative Math can always add a frog such that |S'| ≥ |S| - c for some c < 1, then |S| might not decrease enough.

But let's think geometrically. Suppose S is a vector in the plane. Pepemath can remove any two vectors (after rotation) and Creative Math adds a vector of length 1. Wait, but actually, the removal and rotation affect two existing vectors, and then a new vector is added.

Wait, perhaps think of it as S being modified by Pepemath subtracting z_j and z_i e^{iπ/3}, then Creative Math adds a vector of length 1. So, S' = S - z_j - z_i e^{iπ/3} + z_new.

The question is, can Pepemath choose z_j and z_i such that the best possible z_new (for Creative Math) still results in |S'| < |S| - c, for some constant c, or at least that |S| can be driven down over time.

Alternatively, using the concept of a game where Pepemath can "steer" the vector S towards the origin despite Creative Math's interference.

Alternatively, perhaps consider if all frogs are moved towards canceling the current center of mass. For example, if the current center of mass is C, then to reduce |C|, you want to adjust frogs to oppose C.

But since each frog is on the unit circle, their contributions are vectors of length 1. So, the maximum a single frog can contribute to the sum is 1. Therefore, if the current sum is S, then removing a frog in the direction of S and rotating another frog to oppose S could help.

Wait, suppose S is the current sum. Pepemath's move: remove a frog at z_j (which is a unit vector), and rotate another frog z_i 60 degrees clockwise. Then Creative Math adds a frog at z_new (unit vector).

To minimize |S'|, Pepemath would want to choose z_j and z_i such that S - z_j - z_i e^{iπ/3} is as small as possible, and then Creative Math adds z_new in the direction of that resulting vector, increasing its length by 1.

Therefore, the optimal play for Pepemath is to minimize |S - z_j - z_i e^{iπ/3}|, which would make Creative Math's addition only contribute 1 in that direction, thus making the total |S'| = |S - z_j - z_i e^{iπ/3}| + 1. But if Pepemath can make this expression less than |S|, then |S'| < |S|.

Alternatively, if for any S, there exists z_j and z_i such that |S - z_j - z_i e^{iπ/3}| + 1 ≤ |S|.

Wait, is that possible? Let's think of a vector S. Suppose we can subtract two vectors (z_j and z_i e^{iπ/3}) such that the resulting vector has a norm that is at least | |S| - |z_j + z_i e^{iπ/3}| |, by the reverse triangle inequality. Ah, here comes the isomorphism question. 

The problem considers two trees different if they are not isomorphic. That means that if you can find a relabeling that maps edges and directions, then they are the same. 

In the case of two nodes with an edge from A to B versus from B to A: to check if these are isomorphic, there's a mapping swapping A and B. The edge would then go the other way. Since the direction matters, these two structures are not isomorphic, because you can't have an isomorphic mapping that preserves the direction. 

Wait for example:

Suppose tree1 has edge A→B, and tree2 has B→A. To be isomorphic, there must be a bijection f from nodes of tree1 to nodes of tree2 such that (u,v) in tree1 implies (f(u),f(v)) exists in tree2, and direction is also preserved.

Here, swapping A and B would map the edge A→B to f(A) = B and f(B)=A, so (B,A) in tree1 becomes (B→A), but tree2 has the edge B→A. So in that case, swapping the nodes would be an isomorphism between the two trees. Wait no, if in tree1, edge A→B, then under the mapping f which swaps them, it would require the edge to be f(A)=B, f(B)=A, so edge B→A must exist in tree2, which it does. So yes, the two are isomorphic. 

Wait this is an example. So the direction could be swapped via graph relabeling. Therefore the two trees (edge from A to B or vice versa) are actually isomorphic and hence counted as one structure. 

Hmm. So maybe the two-node structures are only one isomorphism class?

Wait yes. Because the direction can be reversed by swapping nodes. But between two nodes, the only difference between A→B and B→A is the direction, which can be mapped through the swapping isomorphism. 

Therefore, such structures are considered the same. So according to this, the two-node case has one possible structure (up to isomorphism).

Wait, consider case 1:

Nodes A, B, C. Edges A→B and A→C. 

case 2: nodes A,B,C with edges B→A and C→A.

+"""Classes for storing material data."""
+
+import numpy
+from matplotlib import pyplot
+
+
+from serpentTools.objects import _NamedObject
+
+
+class DepletedMaterial(_NamedObject):
+    """Class for storing material data from ``_dep.m`` files.
+
+    Parameters
+    ----------
+    parser: :py:class:`~serpentTools.parsers.depletion.DepletionReader`
+        Parser that found this material.
+        Used to obtain file metadata like isotope names and burnup
+    name: str
+        Name of this material
+
+    Attributes
+    ----------
+    zai: numpy.array
+        Isotope id's
+    names: numpy.array
+        Names of isotopes
+    days: numpy.array
+        Days overwhich the material was depleted
+    adens: numpy.array
+        Atomic density over time for each nuclide
+
+    :note:
+
+        These attributes only exist if the pasers was instructed to
+        read in this data. I.e. if ``readers.depletion.metadataKeys``
+        does not contain ``ZAI``, then this object will not have
+        the ``zai`` data.
+
+    """
+
+    def __init__(self, parser, name):
+        _NamedObject.__init__(self, parser, name)
+        self._varData = {}
+
+    def __getattr__(self, item):
+        """
+        Allows the user to get items like ``zai`` and ``adens``
+        with ``self.zai`` and ``self.adens``, respectively.
+        """
+        if item in self._varData:
+            return self._varData[item]
+        return _NamedObject.__getattr__(self, item)
+
+    def __getitem__(self, item):
+        if item not in self._varData:
+            if item not in self._container.metadata:
+                raise KeyError('{} has no item {}'.format(self, item))
+            return self._container.metadata[item]
+        return self._varData[item]
+
+    def addData(self, variable, rawData):
+        """Add data straight from the file onto a variable.
+
+        Parameters
+        ----------
+        variable: str
+            Name of the variable directly from ``SERPENT``
+        rawData: list
+            List of strings corresponding to the raw data from the file
+        """
+        newName = self._convertVariableName(variable)
+        if isinstance(rawData, str):
+            scratch = [float(item) for item in rawData.split()]
+        else:
+            scratch = []
+            for line in rawData:
+                if line:
+                    scratch.append([float(item) for item in line.split()])
+        self._varData[newName] = numpy.array(scratch)
+
+    def getXY(self, xUnits, yUnits, timePoints=None, names=None):
+        """Return x values for given time, and corresponding isotope values.
+
+        Parameters
+        ----------
+        xUnits: str
+            name of x value to obtain, e.g. ``'days'``, ``'burnup'``
+        yUnits: str
+            name of y value to return, e.g. ``'adens'``, ``'burnup'``
+        timePoints: list or None
+            If given, select the time points according to those specified here.
+            Otherwise, select all points
+        names: list or None
+            If given, return y values corresponding to these isotope names.
+            Otherwise, return values for all isotopes.
+
+        Returns
+        -------
+        numpy.array
+            Array of values.
+        numpy.array
+            Vector of time points only if ``timePoints`` is ``None``
+
+        Raises
+        ------
+        AttributeError
+            If the names of the isotopes have not been obtained and specific
+            isotopes have been requested
+        KeyError
+            If at least one of the days requested is not present
+        """
+        if timePoints is not None:
+            returnX = False
+            timeCheck = self._checkTimePoints(xUnits, timePoints)
+            if any(timeCheck):
+                raise KeyError('The following times were not present in file {}'
+                               '\n{}'.format(self._container.filePath,
+                                             ', '.join(timeCheck)))
+        else:
+            returnX = True
+        if names and 'names' not in self._container.metadata:
+            raise AttributeError('Parser {} has not stored the isotope names.'
+                                 .format(self._container))
+        xVals, colIndices = self._getXSlice(xUnits, timePoints)
+        rowIndices = self._getIsoID(names)
+        allY = self[yUnits]
+        if allY.shape[0] == 1 or len(allY.shape) == 1:  # vector
+            yVals = allY[colIndices] if colIndices else allY
+        else:
+            yVals = numpy.empty((len(rowIndices), len(xVals)), dtype=float)
+            for iso
